Abstract: Prompt learning adapts vision-language models such as CLIP by optimizing a short learnable prompt. The cross-entropy objective pulls this prompt toward
  base-class specialization, whereas generalization to unseen classes benefits from staying close to CLIP's zero-shot feature space. Because both objectives
  act on the same prompt, their gradients conflict at convergence, and in our experiments no single-prompt loss design clearly improves on the cosine
  baseline. We propose Decoupled Mode Connectivity (DMC), which gives each objective its own prompt. Starting from a base-trained prompt, DMC trains a
  specialization prompt and a generalization prompt, and merges their text features with a fixed coefficient at test time, at the inference cost of a single
  prompt. A linear mode connectivity (LMC) corridor in text-feature space keeps the classification loss low for every classifier interpolated between the two
  endpoints, and a Visual Anchor KL term preserves CLIP's zero-shot class-similarity distribution of each image during specialization. We further argue that
  such regularizers should satisfy class-permutation invariance (CPI), and show via Fano's inequality, in an idealized model, that violating CPI
  lower-bounds the drop in novel accuracy by a term proportional to the mutual information between the prompt and the base-class labels. Across 11 datasets,
  DMC improves the average harmonic mean of base and novel accuracy over MergeTune for both CoOp and KgCoOp, and its interpolation curves pass beyond the
  single-prompt operating point, shifting the base-novel frontier rather than trading along it. Code is available at https://github.com/IemProg/DMC.

</details>

Abstract: Online 3D street-scene asset construction turns multi-view video into a continually updated scene of independently editable objects. The central challenge is to accumulate partial, intermittent observations under motion and occlusion without fragmenting identities or mixing evidence across objects. To address this challenge, we propose Video2Asset, an online framework for constructing persistent 3D assets from multi-view video. Using occupancy as an intermediate representation, we construct identity-aware observations with Historical Carrier Guidance for instance decoding and an identity encoder fusing instance-aligned 3D features with multi-view appearance. Accumulated-Evidence Association compares current observations with identity, geometric, and appearance evidence accumulated within asset records to determine identity inheritance and update world-coordinate states. We instantiate meshes from an asset library using these states and combine them with an occupancy-derived static background to support scene visualization, mesh replacement, object deletion, and trajectory editing. Experiments on several datasets demonstrate improvements in temporal identity consistency and asset quality across multiple occupancy models, including a 15.4% reduction in asset fragmentation on nuScenes with SGE.

</details>

Abstract: In this paper, a 3D backbone network (SpconvTransformer) based on the fusion of convolutional architecture branch and Transformer architecture branch is proposed. The convolution branch uses 3D sparse convolution and submanifold convolution to learn local voxel features and semantic information, and the Transformer branch uses the self-attention mechanism to model the remote relationship between voxels, uses the broad receptive field to capture rich context information, and complementing the advantages of the two architectures by fusing the local features extracted from the two branches and the global features solves the problem of limited receptive fields of 3D convolutional neural networks. The experimental results show that the average accuracy of SpconvTransformer in 3D object detection and bird's-eye view detection tasks reaches 81.45% and 88.09%, respectively, and the average directional similarity reaches 91.10%, which is significantly better than other existing algorithms.

Abstract: Event cameras offer high temporal resolution and dynamic range for uncooperative spacecraft pose estimation, but models trained on synthetic event streams must generalize to the noise, background activity, and contrast variation of physical sensors. We present a comparative systems study of two independently developed approaches to synthetic-to-real, event-based 6-DoF spacecraft pose estimation. The first is a modular pipeline that combines learned keypoint detection and unlabeled target-domain adaptation with geometry-based pose recovery and temporal smoothing. The second directly regresses translation and rotation from global and cropped event representations using an apparent-scale cue, physics-motivated synthetic corruptions, and feature-consistency regularization. We evaluate both systems under the same SPARK 2026 hidden-test protocol on the real-event split of SPADES, with predictions for all test frames. The results reveal a component-wise reversal concealed by the aggregate challenge score: direct regression reduces orientation error by 22.7% (1.1767 to 0.9101 rad) and obtains a 13.4% lower composite score, whereas the modular pipeline reduces relative translation error by 78.7% (0.1335 to 0.0285). Archived configurations and a qualitative failure case identify plausible explanations for these complementary error profiles, without isolating their causes. These findings motivate a testable hybrid design that combines direct orientation estimation with geometry-constrained translation recovery.

</details>

Abstract: We present PoseRieFlow, a compact flow-matching framework for text-conditioned generation of whole-body 3D human poses. Prior text-to-pose methods either neglect articulated hands or model rotations in Euclidean space despite their non-Euclidean geometry. PoseRieFlow instead formulates generation directly on the product manifold of SMPL-H joint rotations, learning a text-conditioned vector field that transports samples from an isotropic $\mathrm{SO}(3)$ Gaussian prior to the data distribution along Riemannian geodesics. A part-tokenized Diffusion Transformer aggregates the 52 joints into eight body-part tokens, allocating a dedicated token and nonlinear projector to each hand, and restores per-joint resolution through part-specific decoders, shortening the sequence while improving kinematic fidelity over per-joint tokenization. With only 21.6M trainable parameters, PoseRieFlow supports expressive SMPL-H generation with detailed hand articulation at a fraction of the scale of recent LLM-based generators. On PoseScript, it attains the lowest whole-body kinematic error among all hand-producing methods, with consistent kinematic gains from the Riemannian formulation over its Euclidean counterpart at every practical inference budget, while distributional and retrieval metrics remain comparable between them.

</details>

Abstract: Current solutions for assessing the risk of anterior cruciate ligament (ACL) injury typically rely on expensive motion-capture labs that require substantial resource investment or subjective evaluations of specific athletic stress tests by experts. The Drop Vertical Jump (DVJ) and the Single Leg Hop (SLH) are two examples of such tests typically used to track progress in ACL rehabilitation. In this paper, we develop an unsupervised learning model that runs entirely on a smartphone for two tasks, based on an autoencoder architecture with multiple layers of graph convolutional networks, which utilizes pose estimation data to determine a person's injury risk status. Our model accurately predicts athletes at high risk of injury for both athletic stress tests, with average sensitivities and specificities of 78% and 78% for the DVJ, and 75% and 74% for the SLH. Our findings support the development of a widely accessible smartphone solution for real-time screening of ACL injury risk.

Abstract: Visible cameras fail at night and thermal sensors fail under saturation, blur or loss of contrast, yet most RGB-thermal (RGB-T) detectors mix the two streams with fixed weights and cannot tell when one modality has become unreliable. TriNetra is a reliability-aware surveillance framework built around this problem. The proposed solution computes a frame-level Thermal Reliability Coefficient (TRC) from five label-free image cues, uses it to gate the thermal features of a dual-branch YOLOv8m detector whose visible branch, neck and head are frozen and COCO-pretrained, and passes the same score to a ByteTrack-based tracker that feeds a rule-based behaviour analyser. On the M3FD benchmark (2,940/630/630 split) the detector reaches 87.6% precision, 79.4% recall, 70.0% mAP@50 and 47.8% mAP@50:95 on the test split, with 17.0 M of its 42.9 M parameters trained. TRC stays near 0.78 on clean frames (standard deviation 0.012), decreases with the severity of thermal saturation and blur, reaching 0.53 and 0.69 at full severity, and is insensitive to RGB-thermal misalignment. With identical weights, the adaptive gate matches a fixed gate on clean data (70.0% versus 69.8% mAP@50) but keeps 43.6% mAP@50 when the thermal stream is corrupted, against 38.4%, 29.2% and 25.0% for a fixed gate, full thermal admission and a closed gate; the 5.1-point gain over the fixed gate has a 95% bootstrap confidence interval of +3.6 to +6.5 points. In tracking, the TRC-gated association performs on par with ByteTrack on a synthetic benchmark with exact ground truth and on 14 M3FD clips, and is slightly less stable under thermal saturation, so its measured benefit is confined to the detector. The limitations of the current evaluation are reported together with the results.

</details>

Abstract: Shallow-water UAV imagery provides centimeter-scale observations of benthic environments, but water-induced distortions complicate interpretation. To our knowledge, we present the first systematic zero-shot benchmark of unified multimodal large language models (MLLMs) for both assessing water-induced distortions and restoring shallow-water imagery within the same model framework. We evaluate five open-weight unified MLLMs with frozen pretrained weights and without access to paired reference images or descriptions of the true seabed. The benchmark contains 1,000 image pairs: 600 semi-synthetic UAV scenes created by applying synthetic water distortions to real shallow-water imagery and 400 fully synthetic through-water scenes. Three independent human annotations cover five distortion types. We evaluate four assessment prompts (P0-P3), from direct instruction to structured task decomposition, on all 1,000 images and four diagnosis-guided restoration prompts (R0-R3) on a common 100-image subset. Assessment varies substantially by model and prompt, with systematic prediction biases and a best pooled balanced accuracy of 0.654 for distortion presence. The best pooled PSNR gain is +0.255 dB, no unified MLLM improves pooled LPIPS, and some outputs alter scene structure or hallucinate content. Two restoration-only baselines also show limited transfer: InstructIR achieves a pooled PSNR gain of +0.040 dB, while PromptFix degrades all four pooled metrics under every prompt. These results show that recognizing shallow-water degradation does not imply reliable physical restoration and provide a reproducible baseline for future work.

</details>

Abstract: This paper proposes a novel, lightweight convolutional neural network (CNN) designed for edge device deployment. The CNN is developed to recognize and classify threats or targets of interest. The model is equipped with a real-time hotspot detection algorithm, which enables it to process information quickly and accurately, resulting in improved inference time of up to 20 fps and a detection range of more than 400 meters with typical thermal situational awareness sensors. The proposed CNN has been rigorously tested and validated to demonstrate its high accuracy, sensitivity, and low false positive rate, providing a reliable solution for edge devices to detect potential targets. Additionally, the lightweight design of the CNN allows for easy deployment, making it an ideal solution for extending the security bubble for edge devices. The proposed model can be used for a variety of applications, including surveillance, security, and object recognition.

Abstract: Editing a video stream in real time, without access to future frames, is essential for interactive applications. Existing training-free video editing methods, however, assume full offline access to the entire sequence, while streaming approaches either rely on additional training or are limited to specific editing techniques. We show that this limitation can be removed by analyzing the self-attention dynamics of DMD-distilled autoregressive (AR) video diffusion models. We uncover a key asymmetry: queries and keys remain stable and aligned with their source counterparts across denoising timesteps, while values diverge and encode the evolving content. Inherited from the non-distilled bidirectional teacher, this asymmetry is strongest at high noise, where few-step streaming models operate. The resulting mechanism is simple: a single forward pass suffices to capture source identity in the value features, which can be reused throughout generation. Starting from pure Gaussian noise and injecting only these source values reconstructs the input with fidelity matching or surpassing inversion-based methods, effectively eliminating inversion. Based on this insight, we introduce AR-Edit, a training-free, inversion-free method that caches source values once and selectively injects them during denoising. Across multiple AR backbones, AR-Edit enables real-time streaming editing with superior source preservation and tonal fidelity at minimal overhead.

</details>

Abstract: The extent to which demographic-associated information is detectable across organs and disease states in routine clinical histopathology remains unclear. Here, we analyzed 1.3 million hematoxylin and eosin whole-slide images from 257,322 patients across 25 organs, using pathology foundation-model representations and multiple-instance learning to systematically map information associated with sex, age group, and self-reported race. Across 69 organ-demographic combinations, demographic attributes were broadly recoverable, with pan-organ mean AUROCs of 0.828 for sex, 0.733 for age group, and 0.662 for self-reported race, and substantial variation across organs. Demographic-class balancing produced relatively small changes in performance, while evaluation within diagnosis-text clusters showed modest attenuation. Under training with diagnosis-based grouping, demographic-associated signals remained detectable across multiple organs, including sex in gastric tissue (AUROC 0.865), age group in breast (0.816), and self-reported race in skin (0.728). Literature-guided analysis of these settings generated candidate histomorphological and contextual explanations for further investigation. Together, these findings provide a systematic map of demographic-associated information in routine histopathology, informing investigations of its sources, assessments of potential shortcut learning, and the development of more interpretable computational pathology models.

</details>

Abstract: Purpose: Surgical operative reports support procedural documentation, surgical training, performance assessment, and workflow review. However, extracting meaningful information often requires extensive manual review and clinical expertise. Automated surgical scene descriptions could reduce this burden by leveraging recurring surgical activities and prior knowledge.
  Methods: We propose GRAC (Graph-Based Retrieval-Augmented Captioning), a framework that represents surgical scenes as graphs capturing relationships among instruments, actions, and anatomical structures. Given an input scene graph, GRAC learns to retrieve relevant graph-caption pairs from training data. It compares instrument-tissue interactions between retrieved examples and the input scene, using comparison features to condition retrieved-caption representations during generation.
  Results: On the EndoVis-18 benchmark, GRAC achieved a CIDEr-D of 5.88, compared with 4.23 for SGT and 3.36 for the strongest conventional Transformer baseline. Exact instrument-action-target triplet-set accuracy reached 52.6%, compared with 32.7% for SGT and 26.2% for the strongest conventional baseline. These results demonstrate improvements in language quality and factual agreement. On TORS, GRAC achieved a CIDEr-D of 5.06, compared with 3.80 for the strongest baseline.
  Conclusion: GRAC demonstrates that combining structured surgical scene representations with retrieved examples improves surgical scene captioning and produces more contextually meaningful descriptions.

</details>


### [183] [BEV3SR: Evidence-Aware 3D Spatial Reasoning from Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09816)

Abstract: 3D spatial reasoning from video remains challenging for VLMs because videos provide sequential, view-local observations rather than persistent metric scene records. Many questions must be answered in a query-specified perspective rather than the observed camera viewpoint, and prompting with a fixed set of RGB views may miss sparse or occluded visual cues. To address these gaps, we introduce BEV3SR, a training-free and backbone-agnostic framework for RGB-D videos with camera parameters. BEV3SR turns video observations into queryable metric bird's-eye-view (BEV) scene memory and formalizes each query into an evidence-aware spatial task. The Spatial Task Formalizer specifies the query's reference frame, spatial constraints, objective, and evidence requirement. The evidence requirement determines the appropriate reasoning pathway. A query treated as requiring geometric evidence is handled by the Geometric Reasoner, which deterministically computes the answer using the metric BEV records in the query-specified reference frame. Otherwise, a query treated as requiring visual evidence is passed to the Visual Evidence Aligner, which uses BEV-indexed object records and their associated supporting views to retrieve object-consistent and scene-context RGB frames for VLM grounding. Within a standard 16-frame budget, BEV3SR consistently improves different VLM backbones across multiple 3D scene QA benchmarks, delivering large gains on spatially intensive questions and significant improvements on open-ended scene QA. These results demonstrate that BEV3SR offers an effective, efficient, and interpretable framework for explicit 3D spatial reasoning from video.

</details>


### [184] [Graded convolutional networks: scale invariance without the scale and robustness to unknown blur](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09878)

Abstract: The Gaussian jet of an image is a graded vector space: a dilation by $λ$ multiplies derivatives of order $k$ by $λ^{-k}$. Local graded convolutions are exactly homogeneous differential operators, and a local feature is dilation-invariant if and only if it factors through the scale-normalized jet or, without the scale, through the retraction onto the weighted sphere of the grading. Under unknown Gaussian blur the retraction changes only by a change of scale, whereas the scale-normalized jet acquires a grading factor that no radial normalization removes. At the zero jet the retraction separates image families that radial normalizations and continuous classifiers cannot. Networks built on the retraction are universal for invariant features. On MNIST such a network keeps 88.4% accuracy under a two-pixel blur, against 66.2% for the same network on scale-normalized derivatives; in Lindeberg's Gaussian derivative network the retraction raises accuracy at that blur from 91.5% to 95.1%.

</details>

Abstract: Zeolites are an important class of porous crystalline materials with broad applications in gas storage, separations, and catalysis. Although hundreds of thousands of experimentally realized and hypothetical zeolite structures are known, identifying materials optimized for a target application remains challenging because property labels are expensive to obtain and available only for limited structure-molecule pairs. While prior work has demonstrated that 3D neural representations can predict zeolite properties effectively, it remains unclear which representation-learning strategies are most effective in the low-data regime relevant to materials discovery. We present ZeoBench, a benchmark for self-supervised learning on dense 3D representations of all-silica zeolites, and evaluate the resulting features on six existing downstream adsorption-property prediction tasks, across a range of training set sizes. Our study compares self-supervised 3D pretraining on volumetric zeolite data, adaptations of image-pretrained 2D ConvNets and vision transformers to 3D via multiview and channel-inflation strategies, and 3D ConvNets pretrained on out-of-domain volumetric datasets, alongside hand-crafted descriptors and supervised baselines. Using our pretraining and evaluation framework, we find self-supervised 3D representations outperform existing methods and achieve state-of-the-art performance across both in-domain label regimes and for the unseen domain of metal organic frameworks. Learned representations consistently outperform hand-crafted descriptors, and convolutional architectures are generally more effective than vision transformers in this setting. These results establish ZeoBench as a practical benchmark for 3D representation learning in zeolites and provide guidance for data-limited adsorption property prediction.

</details>

Abstract: Predicting the future motion of multiple people is inherently stochastic: the same observed history admits many plausible continuations depending on intent, coordination, and social context. Yet the dominant paradigm in multi-person 3D motion prediction remains deterministic, with existing methods producing a fixed number of outputs per agent. In contrast, we present a conditional flow matching framework that jointly generates diverse future motions for multiple agents, modeling both global trajectories and root-relative poses. A B-spline reparameterization compresses the generative state space while enforcing temporal smoothness, and a dual-stream transformer conditions each agent's predictions on observed neighbor motion for interaction-aware forecasting. Our method achieves state-of-the-art performance on CMU-Mocap (UMPM) and 3DPW, outperforming deterministic baselines on joint position error, pose error, and final displacement error despite producing a full predictive distribution. We additionally establish the first motion prediction baseline on WorldPose, a large-scale professional soccer dataset, where social conditioning demonstrably concentrates predictive uncertainty along interaction-constrained directions.

</details>

Abstract: Recent years have witnessed remarkable progress in video generation in terms of visual quality and temporal consistency. However, generating videos with precise spatial structure, coherent motion dynamics, and controllable scene composition remains challenging. In this paper, we introduce Video-Coder, the first attempt to train a code-driven video generation agent, which autonomously writes and executes code to construct a dynamic 3D scene (Draft Video) that provides structure and motion signals to downstream video generators. To support this paradigm, we design a dedicated data pipeline and curate two high-quality datasets, Video-Coder-SFT-6k and Video-Coder-RL-2k, which contain diverse prompts and ground-truth reference videos. We further develop the Video- Coder Harness, a unified interactive environment integrating code execution, 3D scene rendering, and execution feedback for agent training and inference. We then train Video-Coder with SFT fol- lowed by harness-native agentic reinforcement learning with dual reward feedback, which combines execution-based and video-based rewards. Additionally, we design an annealing training strategy that gradually reduces the execution reward over time, allowing the model to rely more on the video reward in the later training stages. Experiments show that Video-Coder achieves significant gains, improving Minimax-H3 by approximately 7 points on PhyGenBench and 24 points on VBench-2.0. We hope this work can serve as an open foundation for coding agents in video generation, and we fully open-source our data, models, and code

</details>

Abstract: Safety benchmarks for large language models (LLMs) commonly use multiple-choice question (MCQ) format. However, prior studies documented that MCQ format reduces refusal rates, which may significantly affect the safety behavior of the model. We provide a first mechanistic explanation of this effect on the Weapons of Mass Destruction Proxy (WMDP) dataset across two models - Llama-3.1-8B and Qwen3-8B. First, we use the tuned lens technique to show that when processing MCQ the model commits to an answer-letter token very quickly, which is in contrast to the gradual refusal build-up in open-ended formats. Additionally, we observe that activations on the MCQ format lie roughly 3x further from the refusal direction, compared with their open-ended counterparts. Moreover, ablating the attention heads that carry the MCQ format answer increases the refusal, which is a direct evidence that the MCQ answer-selection competes with refusal. These findings indicate that MCQ safety scores primarily measure knowledge capabilities and do not assess the safety-mechanisms.

</details>

Abstract: Financial institutions construct knowledge graphs (KGs) from shared SEC 10-Ks to serve risk analysis, portfolio management, and investment research, whose analytical needs differ. Extraction must adapt without fragmenting shared grounding, typing, and temporal rules. Hand-authored persona prompts and fine-tuning add cost. Weight-free prompt optimization offers an alternative, but unconstrained evolution of a monolithic prompt can rewrite shared rules alongside persona guidance, obscuring changes and complicating review. We introduce FinEvolveKG, a KG-construction harness separating reusable general and persona-policy blocks and mechanically confining GEPA, a reflective prompt optimizer, to the designated block. During persona adaptation, the approved general block remains byte-stable and every optimizer edit is localized to a separately versioned persona policy. A dual objective balances generic temporal-KG quality with persona-relevant facts, motifs, and multi-hop connectivity. We evaluate five mutation scopes on ticker-disjoint 10-K splits, with three seeds for the persona-conditioned arms (v3-v5) across three personas. On the three-seed mean, the complete two-stage block-constrained workflow (v4) improves over the frozen prompt baseline (v1) by 19.4-30.9% and over the tested unconstrained full-prompt workflow (v5) by 2.2-6.4%, leading v5 on both objective components and winning all nine paired-seed comparisons with the lowest seed variance in every persona. Without further optimization, v4 also exceeds v5 across all evaluated out-of-distribution (OOD) shifts. An SME audit supports using the generated triplets as ground truth; a blinded two-SME audit favors v4 over v5 in 70.1% of contrast-enriched judgments. These results show that mechanically attributable evolution can coexist with strong empirical performance, without attributing the gains causally to the boundary.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have been evaluated on their ability to identify emotions expressed in text and produce fluent emotional language. However, output predictions alone do not reveal how fine-grained emotion distinctions are organized internally. We study seven LLMs on GoEmotions, an English Reddit dataset annotated with 27 fine-grained emotion categories plus neutral. We compare model predictions and emotion-prompted final-token representations with the 28 GoEmotions categories and with coarser emotion-family and sentiment groupings.
  We find a consistent pattern of $\textit{overcompression}$ relative to the fine-grained GoEmotions taxonomy. Under the tested KMeans protocol, when we cluster their hidden representations without using emotion labels, the clearest split is into two groups that largely separate positive from negative emotion. Coarser emotion groupings are also recovered with fewer clusters than the full 28-category taxonomy. Yet linear probes retain substantial fine-grained predictive information. These results suggest that fine-grained emotion information remains accessible even though broader affective structure more strongly organizes the unsupervised geometry.

</details>

Abstract: As AI-generated content spreads into nearly every domain of human interaction, practices and discourses of AI detection, both formal and informal, have become an everyday part of how people interact with text and with each other. In this paper, we explore the real-world practices and discourses of AI identification that have emerged in response to a shifting discursive landscape where the human provenance of a text can no longer be taken for granted. We pair qualitative coding with computational analysis of lexical change, grounded in linguistic anthropological perspectives on language and communication, to investigate AI identifications among English Wikipedia editors and students. We find that these identifications draw on a diversity of evidentiary types, from discrete textual features and judgments about content to circular sense-making claims and presumptions about the author's moral character, and that they are contradictory, dynamic, and contextually dependent. In practice, what often motivates an AI identification is not the presence of any specific textual element, but pragmatic infelicity: a perceived mismatch between the elements present and those deemed appropriate within the text's particular context. Consequently, real-world identifications of AI-generated writing do not merely neutrally point out the perceived presence of "AI-like" features, but can be leveraged as a discursive resource to maintain and define sociolinguistic norms: what can be said in a given context, what is appropriate, and, ultimately, what is human. Further, the design and implementation of systems and tools that treat AI-likeness as a context-free property of text may therefore inherit, and enforce at scale, the norms of whichever contexts they were trained on.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) exhibit vulnerabilities to adversarial perturbations that can induce unintended responses. These vulnerabilities can be identified via intentional attacks and subsequently used to improve model safety. In black-box settings where gradients are inaccessible, existing methods tend to guess adversarial transformations using external knowledge, ignoring the information provided by the target model. To mitigate this, we propose a trainable black-box framework based on reinforcement learning, which learns from the target model to generate tailored adversarial transformations. We demonstrate that our approach achieves lower post-attack accuracy, greater query efficiency and better semantic preservation on both natural language inference and generative Q&A tasks. On natural language inference, it drives post-attack accuracy to 2% within 20 queries. Against generative language models, our trained attacker consistently achieves lower post-attack accuracy than the untrained attacker and wins in 4 out of 6 settings when compared to a strong optimization-based baseline. We further experiment with multiple reward designs and training settings, and we find that no design or setting is consistently dominant. Our code is available at https://github.com/taikunudel/RL-ATTACK.

</details>

Abstract: LLM-based digital twins aim to predict how individuals would respond in new settings from representations of their prior responses. Existing evidence that compression of survey histories need not reduce predictive accuracy suggests that representation structure, rather than information volume alone, may be an important design choice. We study BDE, a fixed representation that organizes each respondent's survey history into Background, Decision procedure, and Evaluation. We evaluate it on Twin-2K-500 (Toubia et al., 2025) by predicting each respondent's held-out answers on 17 tasks. In a paired evaluation with 100 respondents, a fully inferred BDE profile improves overall accuracy over the raw survey transcript by 1.27 percentage points (pp) on gpt-5.4-nano (p < 0.01) and 1.19 pp on gpt-5.4-mini (p < 0.001). Three analyses then examine the schema, extraction depth, and component composition. Against a length-matched free-form profile written from the same transcript by the same extractor, BDE improves pricing accuracy on both simulators (2.15 pp on nano, p < 0.01; 1.38 pp on mini, p < 0.05), while its overall advantage is significant only on nano (1.05 pp, p < 0.05). Allowing deeper inference during extraction yields no statistically significant gain on either simulator. By contrast, adding Evaluation to a Background--Decision procedure profile improves overall accuracy by 1.44 pp on nano (p < 0.001) and 1.22 pp on mini (p < 0.001). These results indicate that the advantage over raw transcripts is not explained by compression or length alone, and attribute it primarily to profile composition rather than deeper extraction.

</details>


### [194] [Semantic Conditional Similarity (SCS): A Formal Mathematical Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08778)
*Mehmet Murat Albayrakoğlu*

Result: 解析错误

Abstract: Semantic Textual Similarity (STS) operates on document pairs, returning a single scalar per comparison. In a multi-document setting where several sources may have contributed to one or more targets, pairwise STS cannot disentangle each source's marginal semantic contribution from their shared vocabulary. In this paper, Semantic Conditional Similarity (SCS) is introduced as a conditional form of STS that computes similarity between two documents after eliminating semantic elements shared with a third via set-theoretic operations. SCS makes four contributions: 1) it introduces directionality, partitioning pairwise relations into lateral (inter-source) and vertical (source-to-target) classes; 2) conditioning induces role-dependent asymmetry by extending the comparison domain from unordered pairs to ordered, conditioned triples of a target, a reference, and a conditioning document; 3) SCS introduces inter-document conditioning by subtracting shared content from a third document, complementing the intra-document conditioning in recent C-STS work; and 4) the set-theoretic decomposition achieves threshold-free partitioning of shared and residual content, requiring no arbitrary similarity cutoff. Although developed for the $n \to 1$ configuration, the framework extends to $1 \to n$ via vertical role reversal and to $m \to n$ via independent composition of source- and target-side conditioning. With the established $1 \to 1$ case, these span the full spectrum of document cardinality. SCS scores should be interpreted ordinally, as rankings of marginal contributions rather than absolute measurements. The broader implication is that metric space axioms commonly assumed in similarity formalizations constrain expressiveness in role-differentiated multi-document settings, and that extending the domain to conditioned triples can recover information that pairwise-symmetric comparisons suppress.

</details>

Abstract: The number of domain fine-tuned models, particularly in the biomedical domain, is rapidly expanding. Fine-tuning promises high-performing models at a fraction of the cost of training a base model, but it can also produce unintended behavioral changes. Multiple studies have shown that safety guardrails can erode with fine-tuning, yet large-scale studies across model architectures, data mixtures, and fine-tuning methodologies remain limited. Where prior work in this space has studied value-centric refusal, this work focuses on knowledge-centric abstention. We audit 25 biomedical domain fine-tuned large language models against 11 paired base checkpoints on MedAbstain and ClinDet-Bench to evaluate the impact of fine-tuning on knowledge-centric abstention. We find that defined appropriate abstention measured by these benchmarks, already low in base models (median 8.3% on MedAbstain), falls further after fine-tuning (median 4.2%, decreasing in 21 of 25 pairs on MedAbstain), and that the magnitude and direction of change are heterogeneous across pairs. These results motivate fine-tuning methods that improve domain-specific capability while preserving appropriate caution.

</details>

Abstract: Recursive self-training of language models, a setting studied in model collapse, can reduce vocabulary while making frequent words, mostly function words, less dominant. We fine-tune English GPT-2 on its own output for nine generations, vary the training-data sampling temperature, and measure every model with one fixed sampler. As training temperature rises, Renyi entropy changes turn from loss to gain at distinct temperatures: min-entropy first, then collision entropy, Shannon entropy and observed richness. Between the outer crossings, word types fall while the most-common word becomes less dominant. Untruncated training at T = 1 lies in this interval: at 13,000 words, generation 9 has 24% fewer types than generation 0 yet collision entropy is 0.91 bits higher. The ordering persists with a finer grid, different measurement samplers, a fixed word budget and five fresh training seeds. Min-entropy again crosses before richness in four further settings (in Hindi only at interpolated roots) and in 12 of 13 distinct measurements under eight text representations. Next-token identities and a categorical simulation isolate decoding and sampling effects; the crossing order remains empirical. Fine-tuning on human text can produce the same split while likelihood improves, and no single order's sign reliably identifies worse likelihood across the tested settings. We recommend reporting entropy profiles at stated budgets with an independent quality measure.

</details>

Abstract: As language agents tackle increasingly complex tasks, they increasingly rely on multiple agents to coordinate specialized work, requiring decisions not only about what each subagent should do but also which tools and workspace resources it should access. Existing agent systems provide mechanisms for controlling tools and execution environments, but do not directly specify the resources required by each delegated subtask. Moreover, task outcomes and execution trajectories alone provide limited supervision for determining whether a subagent has received appropriate permissions. We observe that Agent Skills encode procedural knowledge describing the operations, tools, and files required at each step, providing a natural source of supervision for authorization. Building on this observation, we synthesize tasks and step-by-step procedures from Skills, execute them through subagent delegation in interactive environments, and use verified trajectories to construct training data for task execution and authorization. On ClawArena-Team, our method improves tool permission precision by 13.4 percentage points and workspace permission precision by 24.1 percentage points, while increasing overall task success by 11.6 percentage points for Qwen3.5-35B-A3B. On Terminal-Bench 2.1 and SkillsBench, our method remains competitive with existing agent data synthesis approaches. These results show that the procedural knowledge embedded in Agent Skills provides a source of supervision for precise subagent orchestration that task outcomes alone cannot provide.

</details>

Abstract: We study the problem of causal effect identification from an arbitrary set of observational and/or interventional distributions, which we call Universal Identifiability (uID). The uID problem generalizes existing classical identifiability (ID) and general identifiability (gID) problems, which considered the identification problem from either mere observational distributions or a specific type of interventional distributions. Prior research has established graphical criteria to determine when a causal effect is identifiable in ID and gID problems. Moreover, algorithms that leverage do-calculus and basic probability manipulations have been proposed to compute these effects. Consequently, it has been shown that do-calculus is both sound and complete in these problems. Despite these well-established results, a formal definition of what it means for the do-calculus to be complete does not exist. We propose a rigorous formalization for the completeness of the do-calculus and show that the completeness results in previous work remain valid. However, for more general problems, such as uID, the rules of do-calculus are no longer complete. This is perhaps the reason why the uID problem has remained open thus far.

</details>

Abstract: Named entity recognition (NER) is challenging for social media data in a low-resource language. LLMs often lag behind pretrained language models (PLMs) in NER performance, but they show promise in handling cases that PLMs struggle with. Using a dataset of Nepali tweets, we split the test set into "easy" and "hard" cases using token probabilities from a fine-tuned pretrained language model (PLM). We then evaluate an LLM on the hard test cases using 16 different prompt configurations. We find that selecting examples based on semantic similarity and increasing the number of examples improves F1 score by almost 10 points from the baseline configuration. Although the LLM underperforms the PLM on the full and easy test sets, it surpasses the PLM on the hard test set, resulting in an improved score when the two models are combined.

### [200] [Beyond Truncation? Entailment-Licensed Elision and the Limits of Semantic Preservation in LLM Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08975)
*Mahmoud Alyosify,Mirna Imbabi,Eman Elkhamisi*

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Instruction-tuned large language models (LLMs) answer at greater length than a request needs. We ask whether a training-free controller inside the decoding loop can remove that surplus while preserving content, judged against the simplest possible baseline: truncating the model's own answer to the same length. An entropy-gated logits processor removes 25.5-34.9% of tokens on MT-Bench development questions and 19.9-34.2% on a sealed test split within pre-registered BERTScore non-inferiority margins, yet we prove and confirm by audit that its output is always a prefix of the unmodified answer: the saving is truncation. A survey of 23 training-free mechanism families finds none that selects content better than truncation without oracle labels. We therefore introduce Entailment-Licensed In-Decoding Elision (ELIDE), which, at each sentence boundary, withholds a span from the reader only when a natural-language inference model judges it entailed by the text already shown, while leaving the model's generation unchanged. ELIDE beats matched-length truncation on three model families, but reading 936 of its elisions on 670 public prompts shows that 59% harm the answer, a failure that no automatic measure detects. Scoping the licence to its discourse unit reduces the harmful share to about one elision in ten on two fresh tests.

</details>

Abstract: Code-trained language models produce positive brain scores, which raises the question of whether abstract structure learned from programming languages can substitute for natural-language training. We trained 25 GPT-2-small models on TinyStories, Python, C functions, x86-64 assembly, and the corresponding machine code, and scored them using a shared tokenizer and encoding procedure against matched random-weight models. On Pereira-243 and Tuckute2024, TinyStories exceeds the mean of the four code models by $Δr = 0.053$ and $0.059$ (Holm-corrected $p<0.001$), while code training leaves alignment near the random-weight level. The language advantage is benchmark-specific: Blank2014 shows no corrected language-code difference, and Fedorenko2016 shows no language benefit. Geometry tracks the encoding result on Pereira-243: TinyStories seeds converge on the most similar representations, and its fit-free correspondence with neural dissimilarity peaks at $ρ= 0.14$, twice the code and random-weight levels. Binary-trained models have about three effective dimensions, so seed agreement alone does not imply neural correspondence. Updating only the embedding matrix on English improves mean-over-depth alignment for every code model more than an equal update on its own corpus. Within this architecture and training setup, natural-language training produces a distinct representational geometry with closer neural correspondence on Pereira-243

</details>


### [202] [Failure Profiling and Reachable Trajectory Selection for Reasoning Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09042)
*Yijun Zhu,Jianxin Wang,Chengchao Shen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Training smaller language models on reasoning solutions generated by a larger teacher model is the prevailing approach for transferring mathematical reasoning capabilities. However, the student tends to overfit the output distribution of the teacher and generalizes poorly when its own behavior at inference time deviates from the training data. Recent methods attempt to bridge this gap by incorporating error data, yet the corrective trajectories still reflect the reasoning patterns of the teacher, which the student cannot reliably reproduce, leaving the distribution mismatch largely unresolved. We propose a method, Failure Profiling and Reachable Trajectory Selection (FPRT), built on the insight that effective training data should be jointly determined by what the student fails on and what it can realistically learn. Our method first profiles the failure behavior of the student on each problem through repeated sampling, characterizing it in terms of error rate and error consistency to reveal qualitatively distinct failure modes. Each mode demands a fundamentally different training signal. The diagnosed mode then guides candidate generation from the teacher via distinct prompting strategies tailored to each failure type. Among the correct candidates, our method selects the one whose initial reasoning steps after the point of divergence are most reproducible by the student. These first corrective steps constitute the primary bottleneck, while the remaining steps follow an already corrected context and can therefore be continued more readily. This contrasts with standard reranking approaches, which score the trajectory as a whole and thereby dilute the signal at the critical reasoning transition. Experiments demonstrate that our method achieves the SOTA performance across the five benchmarks on both student backbones.

</details>


### [203] [Does Reasoning Improve Psychological Depth in Large Language Models?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09061)

Abstract: Benchmark gains from reasoning-enhanced large language models encourage the assumption that these models should broadly perform better on complex generative tasks, but this remains underexplored in open-ended, creative domains shaped by nuances of reader experience. We tested this assumption through the lens of psychological depth in short stories. We compared DeepSeek-R1 vs. DeepSeek-V3, our closest available contrast for reasoning-oriented post-training, and GPT-5 vs. GPT-4o, through 60 blinded pairwise evaluations in which seven human annotators and an ensemble LLM-as-a-Judge rated prompt-matched story outputs. Human evaluations showed no universal reasoning advantage: GPT-5 was slightly preferred over GPT-4o (60.0-62.9% across two reasoning-effort settings), whereas DeepSeek-R1 fell behind DeepSeek-V3 (42.9%). Although this pattern was marked by low inter-annotator agreement (Krippendorff's $α= 0.070$), our annotation-quality checks suggested genuine evaluative subjectivity, plausibly reflecting distinct annotator decision schemes rather than annotation failure. In contrast, the LLM-as-a-Judge showed a strong pro-reasoning bias (98.3% of pairs vs. 55.2% of human votes), consistent across its five configurations and partly tracking sentence length. These findings suggest that, at the frontier, reasoning-related quality differences are small enough to be dominated by reader subjectivity, and that LLM-as-a-Judge evaluators can obscure this subjectivity in nuanced creative-generation tasks.

</details>

Abstract: Hundreds of millions of Indians communicate with digital systems in romanized Latin characters -- typing "aap kaise hain" rather than native script -- yet romanized Indic generation is absent from NLP benchmarks and evaluation frameworks. We demonstrate that automatic metrics (BGE-M3, BERTScore) systematically misrepresent the quality of romanized Hindi, Telugu, and Kannada generation. We evaluate four LLMs (Claude Opus 4.8, GPT-5.5, Gemini 2.5-flash, Qwen3.5-35B) on 142 OpenAssistant query-response pairs. On a 72-response diagnostic subset, native speakers rate GPT-5.5 1.43 points higher than Claude Opus 4.8 on a 12-point rubric, whereas their full-dataset BGE-M3 means differ by only 0.004. Two distinct model-specific failure modes emerge: Gemini produces script spillage (16.9% of Telugu responses leak native script), while Qwen collapses to English-only output (16.2% of Kannada responses). We introduce a failure-aware evaluation policy that assigns zero to empty outputs, script-corrupted responses, and instruction collapses -- revealing failures metrics alone cannot detect. Our results show that evaluating romanized Indic generation requires a coordinated bundle: semantic similarity, script compliance, failure coverage, and native-speaker acceptability.

</details>

Abstract: Existing multilingual retrieval-augmented generation relies heavily on learned retrievers and similarity-based ranking from multilingual collections, which are vulnerable to cross-lingual retrieval errors and indexing overhead. In this paper, we explore retriever-free multilingual search, where the model directly interacts with raw corpora through executable shell commands. This paradigm shifts the bottleneck to multilingual search action generation, where exact search is sensitive to entity surface forms and outcome-only supervision provides limited guidance for multi-turn search. To address these challenges, we propose MARS, a multilingual search agent in direct corpus interaction (DCI) with reinforcement learning (RL). MARS introduces corpus-native entity grounding to recover reliable lexical anchors from corpus evidence, mitigating failure to retrieve evidence. Meanwhile, we present a redundancy-aware search reward to encourage concise and effective search trajectories, addressing training collapse in RL of multilingual scenarios. Experiments show that \ours~consistently outperforms strong retrieval-based and DCI baselines, while demonstrating robust cross-lingual generalization and substantially reducing redundant corpus interactions.

</details>


### [206] [AlienBench: A Benchmark of Structural Novelty in Language Model Outputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09094)
*Jonas Oppenlaender,Johanna M. Silvennoinen*

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for creative tasks such as writing, design, and idea generation. For these uses, it matters whether a model's ideas depart from familiar examples or stay close to them. Novelty is one standard measure of creativity, and current creativity evaluations ask human experts or LLM judges to rate how novel an output is. However, these ratings have no fixed reference for what counts as novel (or, in other terms, departure from the familiar). Measuring this departure requires a task with known familiar defaults. The structured imagination task from cognitive psychology is such a task. In this creativity test, people imagine a creature from another planet. The departure from features of Earth animals, such as bilateral symmetry and legs as mode of locomotion, is used as measure of creativity. We introduce AlienBench, a benchmark that adapts the structured imagination task to language models. Models describe alien creatures, and a panel of three LLM judges extracts ten biological features from the description and labels them as either Earth-typical or a departure. The sum of feature departures is the score of a description, from 0 to 10. Each model describes alien creatures under six prompt conditions that vary the framing of the task and the planetary environment. Each model generates 30 descriptions per condition (180 in total), and its overall score is the mean across these descriptions. We evaluate 10 language models, from frontier to open-weight models. Overall scores range from 3.28 (GPT-6 Luna) to 6.55 (Ministral 3 14B). Interestingly, open-weight models score higher than proprietary models in this task (5.40 vs. 4.58). Under the baseline prompt, the models depart from Earth defaults more often on average than 100 human participants on every feature. We release all prompts, generations, judge outputs, and scores.

</details>

Abstract: Vietnamese punctuation restoration is often evaluated on relatively small benchmarks, limiting conclusions about how architectural choices scale with data volume. We present a controlled benchmark on News and Novels corpora containing 22.1 million labeled tokens (19.9M News and 2.2M Novels). We evaluate 17 architectures formed from four pretrained Transformer backbones and optional bidirectional long short-term memory (Bi-LSTM) and conditional random field (CRF) layers, together with a PhoBERT-large-Bi-LSTM-CRF model, PhoPunct. Every model is trained for eight epochs with the same learning rate and without architecture-specific tuning. Under this fixed budget, adding Bi-LSTM and CRF layers does not reliably improve macro F1: in seven of eight matched domain-backbone comparisons, a plain Transformer classifier matches or outperforms its Bi-LSTM-CRF counterpart. PhoPunct obtains the best News result (61.35), whereas XLM-R with a CRF obtains the best Novels result (50.18), exceeding PhoPunct (48.97). These results show that the value of recurrent and structured-prediction heads depends on their optimization regime and motivate explicit separation between architectural improvements and improvements due to architecture-specific tuning. Our source code is available at: https://github.com/aisia-research-lab/PhoPunct

</details>


### [208] [Calibrated concept profiles: making the reading of a model's neurons a measurement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09196)
*Piotr Wilam*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: 解析错误


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 解析错误

Method: 解析错误

Result: 解析错误

Conclusion: 解析错误

Abstract: Interpretability has largely moved from reading a model's neurons directly to reading a second model trained on the first: a probe, a sparse autoencoder, a crosscoder. The move answered real faults -- mixed meanings, thresholds without a unit, readings that did not transfer from one study to the next -- but gave up what a direct reading alone provides: nothing trained in between, the neurons themselves named, and a reading that can be repeated and compared. Here the direct reading is made a measurement.
  A neuron's activation varies with the prompt, so its reading is a distribution over a population of stimuli. We declare that population: every factor around a target construct is drawn at its rate in a reference corpus and logged.
  From the declared distribution we generate a battery of prompts, and draw it twice. From the battery we compute the calibrated concept profile, one number per neuron, stated in units of the neuron's own spread on ordinary input; the two draws give every number a noise unit. Before the profile is read, a fixed set of checks licenses the battery on the model, and the smallest change the profile can resolve across training checkpoints is measured.
  Realised for Python on OpenCoder-1.5B and followed across checkpoints spanning two trillion training tokens, the profile gives what the alternatives do not report: a unit on every number, a chance level for every comparison, and which neurons carry a construct at every checkpoint.
  The battery, the code and the data behind every result are released.

</details>

Abstract: In neuroscience, representational drift is a change in which neurons carry a representation while behaviour stays the same; we measure its analogue in OpenCoder-1.5B during pretraining, for 169 Python constructs over two trillion tokens at a constant learning rate, with a noise unit on every change, at checkpoints as close as 1,000 training steps. For 16 of the 169, those whose grammar fixes a next token, the loss at those positions reaches its floor within the first 4% of training, while the model's reliance on the neurons carrying them grows to the end: removing them costs 0.06, 0.25 and 0.40 nats more than removing a random set, at 4 billion, 84 billion and 2 trillion tokens. Which neurons carry a construct never settles: for all 169 the carrier set is still changing at the last checkpoint, and an abrupt settling at any date from 84 billion to 1.5 trillion tokens, or a gradual one before 500 billion, would have been detected in 99% of injected cases. The set measured at 84 billion tokens, removed at the end, cannot be separated from a random set, so the function is carried by whichever neurons are current. Up close the change is a persisting pool and a changing sample: a neuron that leaves a construct's set returns at three times the rate of a matched neuron, and no set reassembles. The hypothesis we fixed in advance, that the carriers settle, is refuted; we discuss candidate causes and name the experiment that would separate them.

</details>

Abstract: Benchmarks for open-ended generation often rely on large language model (LLM) judges, whose reliability in low-resource languages is uncertain. We introduce Khmer PFGen-Bench, to our knowledge the first judge-free benchmark for multi-sentence answers to questions composed directly in Khmer, a low-resource language. Khmer PFGen-Bench adapts the scorer of PFGen-Bench, a benchmark for Japanese that scores responses with keyword rules and n-gram statistics from LLM-generated references. This scoring requires references that are both valid and varied, which LLMs may not produce in Khmer, so we examine how to build such references. We find that post-trained models can give more valid but less varied references than their base variants, and that mixing model families increases variety for the same number of references. Using references from post-trained models in nine families, the benchmark yields model rankings that are stable across reference sets and consistent with a native speaker's ratings. These findings offer guidance for building judge-free benchmarks in other low-resource languages.

</details>

Abstract: Existing Korean benchmarks for Large Language Models (LLMs) evaluate performance across diverse knowledge domains, but their accuracy scores cannot distinguish whether incorrect responses arise from a lack of required knowledge or a failure to apply that knowledge correctly. Our analysis of existing Korean benchmarks reveals that most questions can be answered through factual recall alone, while those demanding greater application also differ in required knowledge, making it difficult to isolate application ability. To address this, we introduce KLEAR, a framework that constructs knowledge-matched contrasts by varying application demand while preserving the required knowledge. During construction, KLEAR identifies the knowledge underlying each source question as an explicit anchor bundle to guide generation of questions requiring more complex application. Generated candidates then undergo automated and human validation, with iterative revision based on evaluation feedback. Experiments demonstrate that the resulting knowledge-matched sets largely satisfy the intended constraints, enabling controlled evaluation across diverse LLMs. These evaluations reveal that increasing application demand exposes failures that persist even when the required knowledge is provided.

</details>

Abstract: Diffusion language models (DLMs) enable parallel token generation through iterative denoising, yet their inference remains costly due to repeated attention over a growing historical prefix and repeated processing of the entire current generation block. Historical attention becomes increasingly expensive as generation progresses, while all current-block positions are propagated through the Transformer at every denoising step even though only a small fraction are typically resolved at that step. In this paper, we introduce Duet, a training-free dual-sparse inference framework that addresses these challenges through prefix Key-Value (KV) sparsity and early query sparsity, respectively. Specifically, Duet exploits the concentration and cross-step stability of historical attention within each generation block to construct a compact historical KV subset that is reused across denoising steps. For query sparsity, Duet predicts update positions from shallow representations and propagates only the corresponding queries through the suffix Transformer layers, avoiding deep computation at positions unlikely to be updated in the current step. Experiments show that Duet maintains competitive accuracy while achieving 1.34$\times$/2.83$\times$ end-to-end/per-step speedups on LLaDA2.1-mini and 2.41$\times$/4.72$\times$ on SDAR-8B-Chat-b32 at a 32K context length.

</details>

*Lorenzo Pratesi,Eulalia Farre-Maduell,Machteld Boonstra,Michele Miranda,Jonas Mack,Eduard Rodenas-Alesina,Jonathan Kossoff,Corinne Allaart,Sébastien Bratières,Manuela-Daniela Danu,Isabella Fumarulo,Milou Gamage,Antonio Iaconelli,Pavel Leinveber,Felix Lindberg,Pavel Macháč,Vladimíra Macíková,Gianluigi Savarese,Raffaele Scorza,Bram van Es,Emma Weiss,Andrea Guala,Martin Krallinger,the DataTools4Heart NLP group*

Abstract: Background and Aims. Comparable, multilingual, validated cardiology corpora are critical to advance trustworthy natural language processing (NLP) models for cardiology but such resources are scarce due to privacy concerns. This study aimed to cover this gap by presenting suitable artificial corpus creation guidelines and by releasing 3 multilanguage corpora. Methods. Following purpose-specific publicly-available corpus construction guidelines, cardiologists from 7 European hospitals generated artificial clinical records in Czech, Dutch, English, Italian, Romanian, Spanish and Swedish, ensuring clinical diversity and sex balance, resulting in the CliTe corpus. A CliTe subset underwent a multi-stage manual translation into the other 6 languages, resulting in the parallel ParaCliTe corpus, while the remaining reports were automatically translated, constituting ParaCliTe Silver. Results. CliTe is a collection of 699 artificial cardiology reports, mostly outpatient notes (340 documents), discharge reports (263), and inpatient notes (89), describing fictional patients with ischaemic heart disease (199 patients), heart failure (188), and valvular disease (79), among others. ParaCliTe contains reports from 94 fictitious patients, each available in seven languages with sentence-level alignment, resulting in 658 documents. The corpus mainly comprises discharge reports (322 documents), and outpatient (196) and inpatient (140) notes, describing patients with heart failure (399 documents), ischaemic heart disease (287), and valvular disease (238). Empirical results illustrate the utility of ParaCliTe and highlight the limitations of state-of-the-art NLP models adapted to the cardiology domain. Conclusions. The creation of multilingual, equitable and realistic corpora of artificial patients capable of strengthening NLP performance for cardiology is feasible when supported by detailed guidelines.

</details>

Abstract: Self-supervised speech embeddings jointly encode lexical, speaker, and prosodic information, complicating feature isolation. We introduce Asymmetric Correspondence Learning (ACL), distilling HuBERT features into separate Acoustic Word Embeddings (AWEs) and Acoustic Style Embeddings (ASEs) via a split-latent correspondence bottleneck. Through probing and clustering evaluations on single-word utterances, we demonstrate that substantial variance from extrinsic sources remains encoded within each latent space, despite linear probing indicating the information has been stripped. Whilst the AWE space exhibits cohesive lexical clustering (AP$\approx$0.90, Fisher$\approx$6.28), linear probing on the ASE space achieves 97.9\% speaker accuracy across a diffuse topology (Fisher$\approx$1.01), yet preserves substantial lexical variance (Fisher$\approx$0.86) that remains linearly unrecoverable (0.17\%). To explain these findings, we apply group-level permutation feature importance over eGeMAPS acoustic features, finding that AWEs partition by vocal tract filters and ASEs by $F_0$ dynamics. These findings suggest that standard diagnostic benchmarks may be insufficient for verifying feature isolation, as substantial speech structure can be retained even when it remains entirely hidden from probes.

</details>

Abstract: Document-level Relation Extraction (DocRE) aims to identify relations between entities across a document but typically relies on costly large-scale annotations. While pseudo-labelling enables the use of low-quality data, existing methods often suffer from inefficiency and confirmation bias. To address these challenges, we propose Syn2DRE, a semi-supervised framework that combines self-supervised learning with cross-supervision in a lightweight teacher-student architecture to produce more reliable pseudo-labels. Additionally, we introduce a unified end-to-end training strategy to further improve efficiency of the framework. Experiments on multiple benchmarks show that Syn2DRE consistently outperforms prior state-of-the-art semi-supervised DocRE methods.

Abstract: A neural account of lexical access requires an explicit distinction between the structure of lexical representations, the associations linking representational levels, and the activity generated during retrieval. This paper specifies a spiking neural implementation of the mental lexicon within the Neural Engineering Framework and Semantic Pointer Architecture. Conceptual and phonological form representations are constructed recursively from relational graphs, whereas lemma representations provide independent lexical identities. The implementation contains 613 lexical entries and supports two directed processing pathways: perception maps a phonological form to its associated concept via a lemma, whereas production maps a concept to its associated phonological form via a lemma. Each pathway combines two associative-memory variants, winner-take-all (WTA) selection (competition) and thresholded transfer, and uses 199,400 leaky integrate-and-fire neurons. We formalize within-level similarity, cross-level associative retrieval, and the interpretation of decoded activity. Simulations show successful retrieval of eight selected words from the lexicon in both processing directions, with retrieval evaluated against all 613 lexical entries. These simulations use complete phonological forms as input for perception and complete concepts as input for production, illustrating lexical retrieval through the corresponding processing pathways. This paper provides a transparent description of lexical representations and neural retrieval mechanisms, supported by accessible code, lexical data, and online simulation documentation.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models enable fine-tuning-free spoken language understanding (SLU) through in-context learning, but direct query-based retrieval may select semantically similar yet intent irrelevant demonstrations. This paper presents Query-Adaptive Intent Detection via Prototype Extraction (APEX), a two-stage retrieval framework that first selects candidate intents using intent-level prototypes and then retrieves contextual exemplars within the selected intent space. Experiments on the SLURP dataset evaluate joint SLU performance using Exact Match Accuracy (EMA) under full-transcript and perplexity-stratified conditions, with additional retrieval analysis on ASR-generated transcripts. Compared with query-based retrieval, APEX improves EMA by 4.34 and 3.97 percentage points with Qwen3 4B and Gemma-3-4B, respectively, on full transcripts. Under high-perplexity inputs, APEX also yields EMA gains of 7.66 and 2.13 percentage points. Retrieval analysis further indicates that prototype-guided candidate selection achieves higher ground truth intent coverage across multiple input conditions.

</details>

Abstract: We study the effectiveness and robustness of open-weight large language models (LLMs) for Danish offensive language detection, an underexplored low-resource task. We evaluate four model families (Llama, Mistral, Gemma, and Qwen) across eleven prompting strategies spanning five design categories, cross-prompt generalization, Danish orthographic variation, and robustness under balanced and naturally imbalanced class distributions. We compare against three BERT-family baselines with distinct pre-training strategies, evaluated via both masked language model prompting and supervised fine-tuning. Even without task-specific fine-tuning, LLMs can surpass all three fine-tuned BERT baselines, and lightweight finetuning yields substantial further gains. No single model or prompt category dominates, underscoring that prompt design interacts with model pre-training in ways that cannot be predicted a priori. Semantically grounded prompts transfer more robustly, while format-constrained patterns consistently fail to generalize. ASCII transliteration of Danish-specific characters (æ, ø, å) generally degrades performance, indicating these characters encode meaningful signal. At the same time, the gap between a balanced test set (best F1 of 0.84) and the corpus's natural class skew (0.62 trained on skewed data, 0.70 trained on balanced data) -- relative drops of 26.2\% and 16.7\% -- shows that class imbalance remains a critical open challenge. Our code and configurations are available at https://github.com/PASHAADC/Danish-LLM-HateSpeech.

</details>

Abstract: Real-time Voice AI agents must separate the active user from intelligible background speech that survives conventional noise suppression. Background sources include television audio, family members, and colleagues. Production denoisers remove non-stationary environmental noise effectively. Background speech shares the spectral characteristics of the target and passes through, causing false acceptances in downstream speaker verification (SV) and automatic speech recognition (ASR). We benchmark open-source speaker embedders TitaNet-Large, TitaNet-Small, ECAPA-TDNN, CAM++, and SpeakerNet under a strict sub-second latency budget. Evaluation combines a controlled benchmark with a weighted human-annotated production set. Our controlled benchmark is built from real calls in which a second real speaker is injected into silence intervals at modulated amplitude. We report three findings. First, denoising before embedding is generally harmful and raises equal error rate (EER) across architectures. Second, truncating audio below the full voice-activity segment degrades every model. Third, roughly half of the TitaNet embedding is redundant for this task. Trailing dimensions encode population attributes such as language and gender. Such attributes carry little discriminative value in a demographically homogeneous user base. We distill these results into deployment guidance. Verification should run on raw audio with full voice-activity chunks. Thresholds should favor precision over F1. Truncated embeddings reduce the cost of similarity search.

</details>


### [220] [Query-Guided RoPE Modulation for Lost-in-the-Middle Mitigation in Long-Context LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09554)

Abstract: Long-context language models often neglect information in the middle of the input, a phenomenon known as "lost-in-the-middle". Part of this gap may stem from the long-range decay of rotary position embedding (RoPE), which weakens the positional signal between distant tokens. Existing input compression deletes low-importance tokens, and attention reweighting acts only after RoPE has weakened this signal. RoPE-level methods modify positions directly but apply the same remapping to every query. To address these limitations, we propose QGPM (Query-Guided Positional Modulation), a training-free method that reshapes RoPE positions using query relevance from the model's own attention heads. QGPM builds a broad relevance map for lower layers and a sharp one for upper layers. Low-relevance tokens advance by less than one position on fast-rotating planes, pulling relevant evidence toward the query. Slow-rotating planes keep the original layout, with every token in place. Experiments on NoLiMa and RULER with multiple context lengths show that QGPM improves over base models and strong baselines, with the largest gains on retrieval tasks, and generalizes across multiple backbones. Our code is available at https://github.com/iZHpy/QGPM.

</details>

Abstract: Rubric-based reinforcement learning (RL) extends RL with verifiable rewards to open-ended tasks by scoring each response against query-specific rubrics. Under group-relative optimization, however, a rubric that every sampled response fails (unreachable) or passes (saturated) gives all responses the same outcome and no learning signal; in Qwen3-4B medical training, 60.88% of query-rubric observations are inactive. We find that the two states lack different supervision: stating an unreachable rubric in the prompt raises its satisfaction from 1.13% to 91.25%, whereas responses that all pass a saturated rubric still differ in quality. We therefore propose RuACT (Rubrics as Adaptive Critics and Teachers), which assigns each inactive rubric the role that supplies what it lacks. Rubric as Teacher uses unreachable rubrics as privileged information for reward-gated on-policy self-distillation, and Rubric as Critic decomposes saturated rubrics into child rubrics that separate current responses while sharing the parent's weight. RuACT outperforms GRPO, rubric reweighting, and rubric-guided exploration on all four medical and scientific benchmarks with Qwen3-4B and achieves the best average with Qwen3-1.7B. On initially unreachable rubrics, unguided recovery rises from 35.02% to 42.86%, while child refinement improves reward-quality alignment on independent rollouts from 57.74% to 60.77%. Code is available at https://github.com/ZJU-REAL/RuACT.

</details>

Abstract: Language model deployment often requires different trade-offs between competing objectives as user preferences change, yet standard post-training methods typically produce a single policy for a fixed objective weighting. We introduce LambdaMix, a new parameter efficient method for learning a preference conditioned family of policies with group-relative online reinforcement learning. LambdaMix jointly trains one low-rank adapter per objective over a frozen base model. One preference vector selects the adapter mixture that generates responses and weights the separately normalized rewards used to update the adapters. A single training run supports changing preferences at inference without further optimization. We experiment with four models on helpfulness and harmlessness and on solving math problems while following instructions. We also evaluate the two largest models on rewriting comments to preserve wording while reducing clues about personal attributes. On the first two tasks, LambdaMix achieves higher hypervolume than all evaluated conditioned and policy merging baselines across all four models. LambdaMix uses fewer training completions and stores fewer adapter parameters than a set of GRPO specialist policies. With architecture and initialization fixed, varying adapter mixtures and objective weights together gives wider score ranges and higher coverage than varying either alone.

</details>

Abstract: How does using less conversational history change reader-token use and benchmark scores? We examine this question on 1,100 BEAM questions. We held the retrieval ranking and reader constant across five fixed passage budgets, and mean assigned scores rose as the passage budget grew. The score gain per additional token became smaller at the upper tested budgets. Answerable questions generally received higher scores with more context. Pooled abstention scores sometimes rose as the passage budget shrank. We also tested message retention in a controlled task with explicit conversational-state targets and compared retained user messages with token-matched recency. Qwen matched the full state more often for direct requirements when user messages were retained. Its full-state matching declined when answers depended on references to earlier offers. Mistral produced near-floor results across the retention conditions. A scoring audit leaves the correctness of BEAM answers unresolved. These experiments characterize the token and score consequences of the tested memory budgets. Reliable budget selection depends on retaining the evidence each question requires and validating the measure used to assess answer quality.

</details>

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents coordinate through conversation, and the same message can be phrased in simpler or more complex language, bluntly or politely. We test whether such differences change how other agents judge and act, using the LURK Werewolf framework, which separates each utterance's speech-act predicate from its natural-language realization. We rewrite only the realization, check that the game content is preserved, and replay each game day from saved checkpoints against a re-sampled control. We vary linguistic range consistently across a speaker's messages and politeness strategy per utterance. Across 30 games and 4,335 replays, neither manipulation changes the trust other agents place in the speaker, and no effect on votes or night actions survives correction for multiple comparisons; the largest uncorrected effect is that shifting range away from a neutral paraphrase changes about 4 percentage points more of the other players' votes. In natural play, 88% of face-threatening speech is bald on-record regardless of role.

</details>
