Abstract: When should a robot look at the cup, and when at the portafilter? Spatial-reasoning benchmarks evaluate whether a model can describe a scene, but manipulation needs something more specific: the ability to acquire the particular evidence that makes the current command executable. A view that is informative for one task can be useless for another, even at the same workstation. We introduce Seeing What Matters, a benchmark for task-conditioned actionable spatial belief in simulated tabletop manipulation. Across three domains--coffee workstation, drawer/cabinet, and microwave/appliance--the benchmark provides 860 frozen evaluation cases; expanding across methods, budgets, and inference repeats yields more than 115,000 evaluator episodes under two protocols: closed-loop actionability and same-object functional out-of-distribution generalization. Each case pairs a set of candidate RGB views with a manipulation command, shared scripted skills, and hidden postconditions, so the benchmark directly measures whether a view-selection policy acquires the observations its command actually needs. We also provide a diagnostic reference pipeline that maintains a spatial-functional belief state and ranks views by missing evidence. At primary budget settings, this pipeline reaches 98.8% closed-loop and 98.9% functional-OOD success; ablating the functional or command-binding components collapses performance to near zero. Seven vision-language models, evaluated on the same cases, reach only 26-43%. Replacing the reference pipeline's coverage metadata with frozen RGB-derived scores preserves 98.5%/98.9% success; shuffling the visual scores collapses it to 15%/11%, confirming that the result depends on image content. These gaps suggest that spatial belief for manipulation should be evaluated not by scene-level completeness, but by whether it acquires the evidence the current command requires.

</details>


### [279] [Grasp-TAGS: Task-Aware Grasp Selection via Critic Distillation for Prehensile Loco-Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09864)

Abstract: Loco-manipulation, which requires mobile manipulators to coordinate locomotion and manipulation over long horizons, is a key capability for large-object rearrangement in applications such as service robotics and warehouse automation. Despite recent progress, reliably executing these tasks remains challenging because performance depends on complex robot-object and object-environment interaction dynamics. Existing approaches address this challenge either through model-based methods, which often require accurate dynamics models, or through learning-based methods, which reduce this requirement but typically assume a fixed or predefined initial grasp. However, different geometrically stable grasps can lead to substantially different downstream manipulation performance. Therefore, we propose Grasp-TAGS, a task-aware grasp selection framework for goal-conditioned large-object rearrangement. We train a unified PPO-based reinforcement learning policy across multiple initial grasp configurations and distill the learned critic into a lightweight grasp utility network that ranks candidate grasps using only the candidate grasp configuration and goal information. At inference time, we select the grasp with the highest predicted downstream utility for the downstream policy to complete the task, before even making a contact. Experiments demonstrate that Grasp-TAGS improves rearrangement success over both fixed single-grasp policies and random grasp selection, achieving an average success rate of 89.84% compared with 79.87% for the best-performing single-grasp policies, an improvement of 9.97 percentage points. Grasp-TAGS also reduces final orientation errors across all evaluated objects. For more qualitative results, refer to the supplementary webpage: https://rpm-lab-umn.github.io/Grasp-TAGS-web-page/

</details>


### [280] [Locating Contact on Tendon-Driven Continuum Robots From Tendon Measurements Alone via Constrained Moving Horizon Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09877)

Abstract: Tendon-driven continuum robots can reach through constrained spaces, but contact along their bodies is invisible to the sensors at their motors. We show that this is a structural limitation rather than a tuning problem: the map from contact forces to tendon measurements has a null space of dimension at least seven, so no estimator that leaves the contact support free can locate contact. When the obstacle geometry is known, however, non-penetration and complementarity make the contact force locally identifiable, and we prove that the resulting system with unknown contact inputs is locally exponentially strongly detectable. We present a constrained moving horizon estimator (MHE) that encodes these conditions and runs in real time as one quadratic program per sample, and we give a local, mode-wise robust stability result for it. In a finite- element benchmark of a two-section robot pressing and sliding on a wall, the estimator located contact to 0.9 mm and estimated contact force to 0.22 N, compared with 65 mm and 0.72 N for a tuned extended Kalman filter (EKF) with state projection. The estimator tolerated millimeter- level errors in the obstacle position and unmodeled wall friction, under which the projected EKF failed. Under randomized model mismatch over 40 trials, including unmodeled tendon friction, it remained better in force and location in every trial. Without contact, the MHE offered no advantage over the EKF, which confirms that its benefit comes from the contact constraints. This report extends a letter submitted to IEEE Control Systems Letters with complete proofs and additional experiments.

</details>

Abstract: Scenario-based simulation is central to evaluating autonomous-driving policies, but a scenario scripted before execution (i.e., with predetermined, fixed actor routes and speeds) may not preserve its intended interaction once the tested ego policy responds in closed loop. Scripted scenarios cannot adapt to unanticipated ego behavior, while learned traffic actors may remain reactive without reliably realizing a particular scenario intent. We introduce a scenario orchestrator that continuously monitors the joint traffic state and adapts non-ego actors through re-timing, re-routing, and role reassignment for creating a specific scenario intent. At each step, the orchestrator solves a constrained optimization problem that preserves the intended interaction while limiting interventions and maintaining traffic feasibility. We further distinguish scenario realization from policy failure, allowing a test to first be judged based on whether the intended interaction occurred and then by how the ego policy responded. Experiments in the CARLA simulator across multiple scenario families and driving policies show that orchestration realizes targeted interactions more reliably than scripted and learned actor-level baselines, particularly under variations in initial conditions and ego behavior. These results support closed-loop scenario orchestration as a tool for controlled and reproducible policy evaluation.

</details>

Abstract: Recent work on robot manipulation has focused on specialist policies trained on demonstrations. We show that generalist AI models can control manipulators more reliably than specialist policies such as vision-language-action models (VLAs) and world-action models (WAMs). We systematically analyze agentic manipulation policies (AMPs), in which a generalist AI model commands the manipulator directly, adapting its actions as the task unfolds. We present AMP1, a reference harness that runs unchanged across single-arm, bimanual, and mobile manipulation. AMP1 achieves state-of-the-art results on the RoboLab-120 and CaP-Gym benchmarks, with no demonstrations or task-specific training. On RoboLab-120, AMP1 reaches an 85.6\% success rate zero-shot, compared to 49.2\% and 42.9\% for leading VLA and WAM policies. The agent succeeds zero-shot in 104 of 108 real-world trials spanning twelve tasks and three robot instances, reducing failure rates by 20x and 15x relative to strong VLA and WAM baselines. The basic harness supplies no grasping, pushing, or placing skills; the agent develops its own strategies, such as searching for hidden objects, using objects as tools, and calibrating its cameras. With the addition of a persistent memory store, the agent can also learn from experience: an augmented harness with a memory representation that is carried across episodes lowers the failure rate on RoboDojo bimanual manipulation tasks by 4.6x relative to zero-shot deployment. Our analysis suggests that AMPs are substantially more general and robust than specialist manipulation policies, providing a promising foundation for robot manipulation.

</details>

Abstract: Large data centers are emerging as power-electronic loads whose response to grid voltage disturbances can affect bulk-system stability. This paper presents the composition and control characteristics of a proprietary electromagnetic transient (EMT) model of a modern data center comprising an uninterruptible power supply (UPS) with battery energy storage, induction-motor cooling loads, a computer room air handler (CRAH) driven by a variable-frequency drive (VFD), and an aggregated server load. A voltage-based state machine coordinates three aspects of the facility response: the UPS operating mode (eco, double conversion, or battery), the supply path of the CRAH, and a ramped reconnection of the grid-side UPS converter after the disturbance clears. A 100 MW facility is implemented in PSCAD/EMTDC and benchmarked against the PSSE PERC1 aggregate data center model under a symmetrical three-phase fault and low- and high-voltage ride-through (LVRT/HVRT) events. The two models agree closely in their voltage and active-power trajectories, while the EMT model also resolves the converter and motor-reacceleration transients that the phasor-domain model cannot.

</details>

Abstract: This paper studies potential energy cost savings in electric vehicle (EV) charging hubs when users allow cooperative vehicle-to-grid (V2G), and how such savings should be redistributed. To this end, we present multi-objective operational strategies and justice-informed profit sharing methods for a charging energy hub equipped with a battery storage system and solar panels. Specifically, we first frame the dynamic energy hub operational problem aiming to minimize a combination of grid costs and emissions, accounting for time-varying prices and energy mix as well as storage system and EV battery degradation, which is especially relevant when considering V2G operations. We parse the problem as a second-order conic program that can be efficiently solved to global optimality. Our real-world case-study shows the importance of flexibility for hub operation: Equipping a charging station with a stationary battery and leveraging dynamic V2G can significantly reduce the total costs and emissions compared to conventional settings without a battery or dynamic charging by more than 10%. To redistribute these cost benefits to users, we compare standard Nash bargaining with a justice-informed sufficientarian version which caps charging prices to safeguard adequate access to affordable charging. Our results show that the sufficientarian approach better accounts for inequalities in flexibility capital without significantly affecting aggregate costs, highlighting how justice-informed pricing only marginally affects total revenues.

</details>

Abstract: The integration of vehicle-to-grid (V2G), in which electric vehicle (EV) owners can send electricity from their vehicles back to the grid, is an emerging solution to enhance grid resilience. However, V2G deployment in the United States lags behind many other countries. This can be attributed to a broad range of factors, and to investigate them, this study analyzes semi-structured interviews with stakeholders using inductive-deductive qualitative coding. Results from the interviews are coded across financial, technical, social, political, and organizational dimensions in order to understand the three main deployment factors of drivers, benefits, and barriers of V2G. Once coded, the relationships between these factors are used to construct a cognitive map to identify actions that will most effectively drive V2G deployment.
  The results indicate that grid resilience and decarbonization are seen as the main benefits of V2G. User willingness to participate, a lack of technological readiness and standardization, prohibitive costs, and unsupportive laws and policies are among the most frequently cited barriers. Connections in the cognitive map indicate that the most closely related drivers for mitigating these barriers are user compensation for battery degradation, community outreach, the creation of grid interconnection standards and protocols, and supportive grants and policy frameworks for V2G piloting and adoption.

Abstract: In this paper, a computationally efficient robust adaptive Lyapunov-based model predictive control (RALMPC) framework is proposed for a class of constrained nonlinear systems subject to uncertainties and external disturbances. A radial basis function neural network (RBFNN), introduced to approximate non-parametric uncertainties, is incorporated into a control Lyapunov function analysis, together with integral barrier Lyapunov functionals for state constraint enforcement. This analysis yields control and adaptation laws for parametric uncertainties, external disturbances, RBFNN weights, and worst-case approximation error, rendering the closed-loop system uniformly bounded while ensuring convergence of tracking errors to compact sets around the origin. The resulting guarantees are then embedded into the RALMPC optimal control problem through a contractive constraint, thus combining formal closed-loop stability with real-time performance optimization; at the same time, the adaptation laws allow the RALMPC prediction model to be updated online. Comparative hardware-in-the-loop simulations against state-of-the-art MPC schemes validate the efficacy and computational efficiency of the proposed framework. Ultimately, RALMPC is experimentally validated on a custom-built quadcopter platform for time-varying attitude tracking, demonstrating its practical feasibility; comparisons against alternative strategies confirm its superiority in achieving improved closed-loop performance.

</details>

Abstract: Fault-induced transfers of large data centers to backup supply can cause abrupt reductions in grid-served demand. Coordinating reactive-power support to mitigate these transfers requires consideration of facility-specific voltage--duration ride-through characteristics, particularly when support capacity is limited. This paper develops a ride-through-aware extension of quadratic voltage control that assesses voltage deviations relative to the admissible margins specified by these characteristics. We establish four design requirements that jointly express envelope-based severity, duration dependence, and preventive voltage-restoration incentives. Within a multiplicatively gated quadratic class, we derive necessary and sufficient conditions for satisfying these requirements and construct an explicit family of envelope-normalized objectives. We apply the proposed objectives to reactive-power coordination and evaluate their performance on the IEEE 14-bus, New England 39-bus, and NPCC 140-bus systems. Comparisons with Volt/VAR control, conventional nominal-voltage tracking, and recent data-center voltage-control methods demonstrate improved data-center retention under matched reactive-power support constraints. In the IEEE 14-bus study, the proposed controller reduces the mean data-center disconnection rate by 44.8\% and grid-demand loss by 27.4\% compared with the best-performing baseline. These results highlight the benefit of incorporating data-center ride-through requirements into reactive-power allocation.

</details>

Abstract: Battery-less backscatter sensor nodes powered by radio-frequency wireless power transfer are attractive for large or hard-to-reach deployments, but their range is typically limited by the forward power-transfer link rather than by communication sensitivity. We present a method for improving power delivery, and thus range, in dense tag deployments by having nearby tags act as helpers: each helper terminates its antenna in purely reactive impedances, reflecting the power beacon's signal toward a target tag so that it combines constructively with the direct line-of-sight path and increases the power the target harvests. On a hardware testbed, helpers using discrete impedance control are shown to increase the target tag's harvested power by over 50% above an all-absorbing baseline, while randomly set helper impedances give no reliable benefit, and are sometimes even detrimental. We further propose and implement a local algorithm that lets each helper tune its own impedance using only measurements of the target's packet rate, without channel estimation or coordination between helpers, and we show that it brings the target's packet rate close to that of an independently tuned configuration. These results suggest that independent, passive sensor nodes can cooperatively extend each other's power-transfer range with minimal added infrastructure.

</details>

Abstract: Probabilistic load forecasts for AI data centers need to represent both uncertainty under changing workloads and temporal dependence to support grid operations. This paper develops an open, grid-oriented benchmark using measured GPU power from a 448-GPU AI cluster. Forecasts are evaluated through four stages: probabilistic prediction, online uncertainty calibration, temporal scenario generation, and horizon-wide reserve sizing. Following a change in cluster utilization, a quantile LightGBM forecast achieves only 63.4% coverage for a nominal 90% interval, while online regime-conditioned calibration increases coverage to 87.3%. Even with well-calibrated marginal forecasts, independent sampling produces unrealistic ramp distributions and excessive reserve procurement. Reconstructing temporal dependence from historical forecast errors reduces the discrepancy in ramp statistics and lowers realized reserve cost by 7.4-10.6% across three forecasting tasks. These findings show that marginal forecast quality alone is insufficient for operational decisions and motivate joint evaluation of calibration, temporal structure, and decision outcomes.

</details>

The model co-optimizes BESS power rating and energy capacity while allocating capability across three services: (i) demand response over a specified duration, (ii) mitigation of high-magnitude, high-frequency load fluctuations through reserved active power with zero net state-of-charge change, and (iii) voltage support via reactive power capability at the point of common coupling. Minute-level operational scheduling enforces state-of-charge feasibility and load ramp constraints, and incorporates a controllable queue of delay-insensitive computing tasks to enhance flexibility. Abstract: We consider nonlinear systems whose measured output is the product of an unknown matrix and a known saturation function of the unmeasured state. Generating excitation that allows the matrix to be recovered from the output is challenging when the dynamics are largely unknown. We exploit saturation to construct this excitation. Under a geometric accessibility condition, we derive explicit open-loop controls that drive the state into regions where the saturation takes prescribed values. For componentwise saturations, this condition holds generically with as few as two independent control directions. The controls require no state measurement and only partial knowledge of the dynamics, with no information on the structure of its nonlinear part. The resulting regressor is known in advance, and the unknown matrix is recovered exactly by a closed-form regression. Under stronger structural assumptions and with sufficiently many independent outputs, an output feedback tracks prescribed reference trajectories within the unsaturated region to arbitrary accuracy and permits exact state recovery. Suitably chosen references then also enable exact identification of linearly parameterized (nonlinear) dynamics. We analyze robustness to measurement noise and illustrate the method numerically in the presence of both measurement and dynamical noise.

</details>

Abstract: The ENTSO-E Transparency Platform publishes generation, load, and cross-border power flows for European bidding zones. The values carry undocumented errors and violate energy conservation, and data reconciliation consists in correcting them to satisfy that balance. In this work, we formulate data reconciliation as Bayesian inference. Classical methods implicitly take a uniform prior, which we replace with a diffusion model estimated from the observations alone by empirical-Bayes expectation-maximization. The resulting posterior is sampled with a guided process whose projection step makes every sample satisfy the balance exactly. On synthetic systems of six to thirty-five bidding zones, built from ENTSO-E resource-adequacy data, the diffusion prior gives the most accurate reconciliation, increasingly so as the system grows. Its credible intervals are calibrated or conservative, and it remains the most accurate under a misspecified noise covariance. Finally, when the generation of entire zones goes unreported, the classical methods fail while the learned prior recovers it from cross-zone structure.

</details>

However, the remaining isolated converters must directly accommodate a wide output-voltage range. This paper investigates variable dc-link voltage operation of a single-isolation-stage multioutput SST to ease this requirement. Coordinated cascaded H-bridge modulation and embedded four-switch balancing circuits support intentionally unequal submodule dc-link voltages, allowing the isolated-converter input voltages to be adjusted according to the output requirements. The achievable voltage range is determined by relating the required balancing power to the BC transfer capability within the available CHB switching windows, subject to modulation and peak and rms current limits. The analysis quantifies the range extension provided by BC operation beyond modulation alone. For a single-phase-shift dual-active-bridge converter, input-voltage following within this range reduces the calculated peak current and extends the ideal zero-voltage-switching region compared with fixed input. Experiments on a six-submodule SST prototype demonstrate unequal dc-link voltage operation and inter-submodule power exchange. Experiments result on DAB demonstrate reduced current stress and improved efficiency with voltage following.

Abstract: Electric-vehicle dc fast-charging stations and data centers require multiple high-power dc outputs with independently varying demands. Conventional multi-output solid-state transformers typically establish a common dc bus and use downstream dc/dc converters for port-level regulation and, where required, galvanic isolation. A single-isolation-stage solid-state transformer can eliminate this additional high-power interface stage by directly forming multiple isolated outputs from its submodule dc/dc stages. However, because one submodule is generally insufficient for an individual output port, multiple submodules must be connected in parallel, while inter-submodule balancing circuits redistribute power under unbalanced loading. The submodule grouping therefore determines the internal balancing-power paths. Sequential grouping can create long transfer paths and accumulated balancing power, increasing current stress and losses. This paper proposes an optimal power-flow grouping topology that interleaves submodules assigned to different output ports, thereby localizing power redistribution and reducing the power processed by the balancing circuits. Analytical models quantify the required balancing power and its reduction relative to sequential grouping. A coordinated submodule-bypass strategy is also developed, and the remaining output-power capability under different fault scenarios is evaluated. A six-submodule prototype validates the proposed topology. Under the same highly unbalanced operating conditions, the proposed topology reduces the maximum submodule dc-link voltage difference by 63\% and the peak balancing current by 14.5%, while improving the measured efficiency by approximately 5.6% at rated power. Stable post-fault operation is also experimentally demonstrated.

</details>


### [296] [An Inspection Game for Monitor Design Against GNSS Timing Attacks in Synchronous Islanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09331)

Abstract: Phasor measurement units (PMUs) take their time from GNSS, so a spoofer that slowly drags a receiver clock rotates the reported phase without touching the power waveform. The threat is acute in synchronous islanding, where a controller keeps an islanded generator in phase with the grid, using one PMU on each side of an open breaker, so that the two can be reconnected at any moment: a differential clock bias of about $0.56$\,ms can make the controller hold the generator $10^\circ$ out of phase while it reports perfect synchronism. We cast the defense as a zero-sum inspection game. The defender, as inspector, chooses its timing monitors and audits the clocks periodically; the attacker, as inspectee, drags the two clocks to an unsafe bias by the next audit; the payoff is the evidence, the Kullback--Leibler divergence between the probability laws of the defender's record under attack and under normal operation. Our central result is that the evidence equals the cost of the cheapest innocent explanation of the attacked record. This single identity predicts which monitors work, turns the attacker's best response into a strictly convex quadratic program, and turns the defender's design into a convex program with a saddle point. Pairing explanations, which bound the evidence from above, with test statistics, which bound it from below, certifies audit periods in which every successful attack leaves at least a threshold of evidence. On a 50\,Hz island, these evidence-based periods, at the matched-test threshold, are up to 8.4 times as long as those of a rule that checks each monitor separately, and the case study favors placing the better oscillator on the utility side of the breaker.

</details>

Abstract: A spiking model, called the High-Pass (HP) model, has recently been proposed as a minimal excitable primitive. This model is composed of a static activation function, usually a sigmoid, in positive feedback with a high-pass filter. The HP model enters a tonic spiking regime whenever its input lies in the high-gain zone of the sigmoid, where the sigmoid's slope exceeds 1. In neuroscience, the frequency-current (FI) curve is the standard way of characterizing the tonic spiking regime of a neuron. So far, the FI curve of the HP model has been characterized analytically only for specific sigmoids, and has otherwise been computed through simulation. These simulations are computationally heavy, since simulating neurons requires solving a stiff system of differential equations. This paper bridges this gap by analytically characterizing the FI curve of the HP model with a first-order high-pass filter, for a broad class of sigmoid functions. The FI curve of the first-order HP model can be computed by solving two input-independent equations and evaluating two input-dependent integrals, with no need to simulate the model itself. A spiking primitive is said to be Type I (resp. Type II) if tonic spiking starts at a zero (resp. non-zero) frequency. This paper also shows that, for the HP model, the type depends solely on the local shape of the sigmoid at the outer edges of its high-gain zone. A sharp corner, where the derivative approaches a value strictly smaller than 1 from the outside, yields a Type I primitive. A locally twice differentiable corner, with a bounded second derivative and a derivative approaching 1 at the edge, yields a Type II primitive.

</details>

Abstract: Conductance-based models are central to neurophysiology and neuromorphic engineering, yet their simulation scales poorly to large networks. We study and analyze an iterative solver that operates in signal space. Each iteration reduces to $N$ decoupled scalar linear time-varying differential equations, one per neuron, which are solved in parallel and in closed form. The solver admits two complementary interpretations: a variable metric forward-backward splitting method, rooted in operator theory, and a waveform relaxation quasi-Newton method, rooted in circuit simulation. We prove uniform and superlinear convergence, with a bound that reflects the conductance structure of the dynamics. The algorithm is illustrated on the classical Hodgkin--Huxley neuron and on networks of up to 5000 neurons.

</details>

Abstract: Electro-hydraulic pitch systems account for about one fifth of wind-turbine failures, motivating the development of resilient control strategies for fault mitigation. The study considers a large set of robust nonlinear control designs of different feedback principles and architectures, and assesses their fault-tolerant capabilities in comparison to each other and against the industrial standard, i.e. the Proportional-Integral (PI) controller. The implementation and tuning of each scheme is based on a high-fidelity simulator to ensure nominal performance, whereas each controller is deployed unchanged on an experimental apparatus that includes a full-scale electro-hydraulic pitch system. The comparative evaluation of the considered fault-tolerant solutions is carried out through several tests that cover nominal operation and twenty-five representative fault scenarios, including sensor, process, and actuator faults. The analysis highlights the trade-off between performance preservation under faults and increased actuator chattering. The experimental results show that introducing a dynamic input-reconstruction module into the design of the nonlinear controllers significantly reduces actuator chatter without compromising their fault-tolerant properties.

</details>

Abstract: The article presents a nonlinear adaptive control strategy for a class of feedback-linearizable systems subject to process faults and disturbance. The controller is derived through Lyapunov analysis and incorporates an adaptive proportional gain within a PD-like structure, preserving simplicity while providing nonlinear adaptive behavior. A residual-based gating mechanism activates the adaptation only when the tracking error exceeds a prescribed threshold, preventing unnecessary gain updates during nominal operation and improving robustness to noise. The proposed design yields uniform global ultimate boundedness of the closed-loop signals, with explicit expressions for the steady-state tracking bound. Practical tuning guidelines for the adaptive parameters and Lyapunov matrix selection are provided to ensure consistent gating behavior. Simulations on a precision positioning system illustrate effective compensation of process faults, rapid gain escalation during degraded conditions, and smooth reversion to nominal performance, demonstrating that the method enhances fault tolerance while retaining the structural simplicity of PD control.

Abstract: The small-signal stability of a power system model with arbitrary loads is investigated, with a focus on locally constant power loads in lossless networks. A central element of the proposed analysis is the matrix H, which represents the partial derivatives of generator powers with respect to their relative angles. The local stability of any equilibrium point is determined by the properties of H if the loads consume locally constant power. Specifically, an equilibrium point is asymptotically stable if H is positive definite and unstable if H is full-rank and not positive definite, while several quantitative relationships between the eigenvalues of H and the system Jacobian matrix are also established. The main contribution is that loads are allowed to be arbitrarily located within the transmission network, thereby generalizing the existing H matrix stability criterion which is valid only if loads are located at the same buses as the generators. The advantage of the proposed criterion is demonstrated through case studies involving systems with up to 2869 buses. Accurate stability assessments are maintained, where alternative methods, such as the NRPS model, are found to be overly conservative. Additionally, a computational acceleration by a factor of 340 relative to Jacobian-based methods is achieved.

</details>

Abstract: Current sea ice mapping benchmarks are mostly built around single-sensor SAR products, limiting the evaluation of cross-sensor variability and the effectiveness of combining observations from different SAR platforms. Models trained on such datasets may also be sensitive to sensor-specific artifacts, radiometric characteristics, and acquisition conditions, which can reduce robustness and limit generalization. To address these limitations, this study introduces Arctic-SAR, a cross-sensor SAR dataset for Arctic sea ice mapping, constructed by matching RADARSAT Constellation Mission (RCM) dual-polarization imagery with Sentinel-1 scenes from the AI4Arctic Sea Ice Dataset. The dataset consists of 221 co-registered scenes and supports multi-task prediction of sea ice concentration (SIC), stage of development (SOD), and floe size (FLOE). In addition to the dataset, we establish a benchmark for cross-sensor SAR sea ice mapping by quantifying the contribution of RCM imagery and evaluating single-sensor baselines alongside representative input-, feature-, and decision-level fusion strategies within a consistent multi-task U-Net framework. Across three independent runs, the test results show that the effectiveness of cross-sensor fusion is strongly dependent on the fusion strategy and prediction task. Decision-level fusion achieves the highest mean combined score of $77.28\pm0.29\%$, essentially matching the Sentinel-1-only baseline at $77.26\pm0.13\%$, while achieving the highest SIC \(R^2\) score. These findings show that Sentinel-1 and RCM provide task- and class-dependent information, but that simple joint cross-sensor fusion does not consistently improve generalization over a strong single-sensor baseline. Arctic-SAR therefore provides a benchmark for cross-sensor SAR fusion research in automated Arctic sea ice mapping.

</details>

Abstract: Training prostate lesion segmentation models on biparametric MRI (bpMRI) is limited by small datasets with heterogeneous imaging acquisition protocols and annotations. In this work, we train a segmentation model on a combined cohort of 1,579 lesion-containing training cases from five datasets, representing, to our knowledge, the largest collection of prostate lesion annotations used for this task.
  Additionally, we investigate two complementary improvements to the widely used nnU-Net framework. First, we introduce a random wavelet augmentation and show that random sym4 (Symlet) wavelets outperform random convolution augmentation in terms of average Dice score, with only a modest reduction in F1 score due to lower precision. Second, we evaluate instance-level losses and show that Connected-Component-based Dice (CC-Dice)-CE loss complements BackSplit, yielding better performance than BackSplit alone.
  Together, these improvements achieve the best overall performance, reaching a Dice score of 0.614 on the public Prostate158 test set and establishing a new state-of-the-art on this benchmark.
  We publicly release our training code and model weights.

Abstract: Whether breast-specific mammographic aging provides information beyond chronological age for future cancer prediction remains unclear. We propose SideAge-RiskNet, a framework integrating predicted mammographic-age features with breast-specific representations for multi-year breast cancer risk and conditional laterality prediction. A side-aware DenseNet121 trained on never-cancer reference examinations estimates left and right breast ages. Age-adjusted residuals and bilateral age descriptors are subsequently fused with corresponding image representations, while clinical variables contribute through an image--clinical risk branch. The framework jointly predicts cumulative cancer risk over 1--5 years and the subsequently affected breast among unilateral cancer cases. Across 4,480 EMBED and 248 external examinations, pre-cancer scans demonstrated pronounced bilateral age asymmetry. SideAge-RiskNet established state-of-the-art performance in 1--5-year risk assessment over Mirai and AsymMirai with mean risk AUROCs of 81.73% on EMBED and 72.83% on CSMC. Furthermore, it enabled conditional cancer laterality prediction, achieving AUROCs of 73.26% and 82.53%, respectively. This demonstrates the critical utility of bilateral aging phenotypes in localized breast cancer risk surveillance.

</details>

Abstract: Supervised deep learning segmentation models, although unparalleled in performance, are not immune to anatomical mistakes. We develop a segmentation-by-generation paradigm extending the Nouveau VAE (NVAE) generative framework for anatomically correct cardiac segmentation. In particular, we propose a novel conditional NVAE model (CNVAE) for conditional shape generation, and explore its capabilities for image segmentation in two scenarios: (1) direct image-to-mask CNVAE for one-step segmentation; (2) mask-to-mask CNVAE for refinement of segmentation masks produced by third-party segmentation models. Both models are lightweight and return consistently anatomically valid segmentation masks (> 98% success rate, an improvement of 15% above state-of-the-art segmentation models) on the cardiac segmentation/editing tasks of interest, without loss of accuracy in segmented contours.

</details>

Abstract: Brain tumour segmentation from magnetic resonance imaging normally assumes that all four imaging sequences are available, but in clinical practice one or more are frequently missing. Existing methods for incomplete inputs learn modality-invariant representations or distil from a full-modality teacher, and return a single deterministic mask. Gen- erative formulations instead model a distribution over plausible masks, but the cost of sampling at full resolution has largely confined them to two dimensions. This paper proposes a three-dimensional conditional latent diffusion framework for whole-tumour segmentation from incom- plete multi-sequence MRI. Images and annotations are first compressed by two volumetric autoencoders, which makes diffusion over whole vol- umes affordable, and sequences are dropped at random during training so that a single model handles every combination of missing sequences. Because the model samples rather than predicts, repeated draws also yield a voxel-wise map of disagreement at no extra training cost. Evalu- ations on 2,601 studies from BraTS 2023 and 2024 show that the method reaches 0.955 Dice with all four sequences and 0.904 with FLAIR alone, and that it is the most accurate of the four methods compared in eight of the fifteen configurations. The disagreement between draws widens as sequences are withheld, giving a signal that accompanies the prediction rather than replacing it

</details>


### [307] [RapidMitDet: Boosting Computational Efficiency for Mitosis Detection via Magnification Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08542)

Abstract: Automated mitosis detection is a critical research focus, enabling pathologists to rapidly assess mitotic indices for tumor malignancy evaluation. However, most of the existing detection approaches suffer from significant computational inefficiency due to the giga-pixel image resolution of Whole-Slide Images (WSIs), which greatly hinders their clinical applicability. In this study, we propose RapidMitDet, a computational efficient mitosis detection approach. Our method employs a coarse-to-fine framework, first identifying mitotic candidates at lower magnification and then confirming them at higher magnification. In the coarse-detection stage, we introduce magnification-aligned knowledge distillation to reduce the information gap between magnification levels while minimizing the performance degradation in the lower magnification detection model. In the subsequent fine-classification stage, we propose adaptive triplet learning (ATL) to optimize infomative sample selection, thereby enhancing discrimination between mitotic and non-mitotic instances. Experimental results show that RapidMitDet achieves the optimal accuracy-efficiency balance at 20x magnification, improving computational speed by up to 5.62 times over the latest state-of-the-art (SOTA) methods. Due to its lightweight model design, RapidMitDet can also deliver efficient detection at higher magnification (40x) while achieving even higher detection performance. In WSI-level evaluations, our method shows strong agreement with pathologists' diagnoses (ICCs: 0.879 (40x), 0.822 (20x)) while maintaining superior efficiency, highlighting its potential for clinical application. Code is available at https://github.com/logincat/RapidMitDet

</details>


### [308] [GenStrain: Multi-Conditional Generative Diffusion Model To Synthesize High-quality Motion and Strain Maps From Cardiac Magnetic Resonance Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08544)

Abstract: This paper presents GenStrain, a novel method for synthesizing high-quality cardiac motion-based strain maps from routinely collected cine cardiac magnetic resonance (CMR) images. Existing approaches typically rely on image registration to estimate motion (a sequence of displacements) from cine CMR images, but cine CMR presents very sparse trackable intramyocardial features, presenting challenges to estimate intramyocardial motion. An advanced imaging technique, Displacement Encoding with Stimulated Echoes (DENSE) CMR, is considered as the gold standard for strain assessment, but it requires additional acquisitions, which are not ideal for efficient clinical workflows. To address this gap, we introduce GenStrain, a novel framework designed to learn the data distribution of highly accurate cardiac motion captured by DENSE. In particular, we develop a diffusion process in motion space, where spatiotemporal continuity is carefully considered. To fully leverage both cine CMR images and their associated motion patterns, we introduce a multi-conditional conditioning strategy that integrates anatomical features and motion representations learned by a spatiotemporal registration model to guide the generation process. Experimental results demonstrate that our proposed model, GenStrain, outperforms existing strain analysis methods based on displacement estimates from both traditional registration approaches and state-of-the-art (SOTA) deep learning models. Our model represents a promising step toward more accurate cardiac function assessment and holds great potential for clinical translation. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/Rishov-MIA/GenStrain.

</details>

Abstract: No-reference image quality assessment (IQA) of low-dose CT predicts the score that radiologists would assign to a slice without a full-dose reference, as needed to compare dose protocols and denoisers. Trained CT IQA networks agree well with radiologists but output only a score, whereas multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can explain a score but perform poorly on CT when only prompted. We propose a measurement-aware MLLM, built on Qwen3.8-27B, that rates a slice from its image together with six image-domain measurements of noise, sharpness, high-frequency content and streaks, given to the language model as percentiles in text. On the LDCT-IQA 2023 test set, it reaches an Overall score of 2.735, on par with the retrained challenge winner (2.735) and MedSigLIP-FiLM (2.713), and substantially above its base MLLM used as a zero-shot agent. Shuffling the measurements at test time reduces the score by 0.048, mostly through noise, but the same measurements also lead the model to assign higher scores to over-smoothed denoised images.

</details>

Abstract: Discerning core performance bottlenecks in hyperspectral target detection is highly challenging given the complex interplay between sub-systems of the imaging chain (scene, atmosphere, sensor and processing). Prior sensitivity and limitation studies offer narrow insights applicable to specific system configurations. To address the need for broader generalization, an analysis framework was constructed to quantify limitations from large ensembles of model scenarios. Key components of the framework include the use of canonical scene representations and the log-weighted area under the ROC curve (wAUC), a metric well-suited for rare target detection. By analyzing thousands of scenarios, parametric thresholds were quantified where detection performance degrades, corresponding to "knees" in performance curves. A knee significance score (KSS) is introduced to assess the relative importance of these thresholds. The framework an airborne VNIR sensor implanted in 28,000 scenarios using seven system parameters spanning the scene, atmosphere and sensor. Results suggest that aerosol visibility in the 2-6 km range is the dominant limiter in detection, followed by subpixel target percentages of 15-35%, and background clutter modeled using a t-distribution with 3-5 degrees of freedom.

</details>

Abstract: This paper proposes a transmitter-side semantic extraction framework for goal-oriented Vehicle-to-Everything (V2X) communication supporting safe intersection passage. Instead of transmitting raw sensory data, roadside infrastructure extracts task-relevant semantic information from video and traffic-signal sequences and organizes them into compact semantic packets for low-latency driving-decision assistance. The intersection-passing problem is formulated as a set of traffic tasks under Japanese traffic regulations, and the proposed pipeline generates perception and intent semantics through object detection, object tracking, region-of-interest (ROI)-based semantic extraction, and recommended-action generation. The framework is implemented and evaluated on a self-constructed, CARLA-based single-vehicle dataset collected under clear weather conditions. Experimental results show high recommended-action accuracy within the tested task set, while the mean processing latency remains below the frame interval of the 15~fps input video.

Abstract: Coronary ostia and centerlines are key reference points for automated analysis of coronary computed tomography angiography (CCTA), from surgical planning to intraoperative vessel tracking. However, the only public ostium annotations cover 77 non-gated aortic scans, not CCTA, while CAT08 and ASOCA (32 and 40 CCTA scans) mark the ostia only implicitly, as centerline start points. This work enriches ImageCAS, the largest public dataset for coronary artery segmentation, with ostium annotations for its 1000 patients and centerline graphs rooted at these new ostia, making it, to the authors' knowledge, the largest public dataset for ostium localization and the only one with centerlines following the official ImageCAS masks and starting at annotated ostia. Three annotators marked each ostium in the first 500 volumes; in the next 500, the candidates of a model trained on the first batch were corrected (human-in-the-loop). Six 3D localization models, trained on ImageCAS, CAT08 and ASOCA, were benchmarked on 58 held-out volumes (50 ImageCAS, 4 CAT08, 4 ASOCA). SwinUNETR-V2 and nnResUNet localized all ostia without failure (>5 mm error), with median errors of 0.70-0.87 mm. Trained on CAT08 and ASOCA alone, SwinUNETR-V2 fails on 34% of right and 53% of left ostia, whereas adding 250 ImageCAS volumes removes all failures. The centerlines were extracted by a deterministic, quality-controlled minimal-path procedure; on ASOCA, they cover a median of 99.5% of the reference length, with a median per-patient mean distance of 0.22 mm between matched reference points and centerlines. Annotations, centerlines, code, and weights are available at https://hal9000-lab.github.io/.

</details>

Abstract: Standard computed tomography (CT) reconstruction algorithms such as filtered back projection (FBP) and Feldkamp-Davis-Kress (FDK) require many views for producing high-quality reconstructions, which can slow image acquisition and increase cost in non-destructive evaluation (NDE) applications. Over the past 20 years, a variety of methods have been developed for computing high-quality CT reconstructions from sparse views. However, the problem of how to select the best views for CT reconstruction remains open. In this paper, we present a novel view covariance loss (VCL) function that measures the joint information of a set of views by approximating the normalized mean squared error (NMSE) of the reconstruction. We present fast algorithms for computing the VCL along with an algorithm for selecting a subset of views that approximately minimizes its value. Our experiments on simulated and measured data indicate that for a fixed number of views our proposed view covariance loss selection (VCLS) algorithm results in reconstructions with lower NRMSE, fewer artifacts, and greater accuracy than current alternative approaches.

</details>


### [314] [AdaptivePnP: Fast Auto-Tuned Plug-and-Play for Deconvolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09845)
*Haley Duba-Sullivan,Emma J. Reid,Charles A. Bouman,Gregery T. Buzzard*

Main category: eess.IV

Abstract: Plug-and-play (PnP) methods reconstruct images by alternating a data-fitting step with an image denoiser. Their performance depends on two parameters: the step size $γ$ and the measurement noise level $σ$. In practice, $γ$ is often tuned by grid search over validation images and must be retuned whenever the problem setting changes, and $σ$ is assumed known or estimated separately. We propose AdaptivePnP, a PnP framework that jointly estimates $σ$ and $γ$ at every iteration from the data-fitting residual. To do so, we apply the PnP virtual model to the data-fitting proximal map and show that the residual is zero-mean Gaussian with covariance parameterized by $γ$ and $σ$. However, when one parameter is much larger than the other, the residual carries little information about the smaller one and using the maximum likelihood (ML) estimator can degrade the PnP iterations. We therefore introduce the identifiable maximum likelihood (IML) estimator, which restricts the ML estimator to an identifiable window in which the normalized Cramér--Rao bound of every parameter is bounded by a user-specified tolerance. For the virtual model, this window is an interval of ratios $ρ= γ/σ$, so computing the IML estimate reduces to a one-dimensional search over $ρ$. We apply AdaptivePnP to two standard PnP methods, yielding algorithms that require neither a tuned $γ$ nor a known $σ$. On image deblurring and super-resolution, AdaptivePnP matches or outperforms PnP baselines tuned with the true $σ$. Code is available at https://github.com/hdubasullivan/AdaptivePnP.

</details>


### [315] [Super-resolved digital terrain models for GEDI geolocation correction: agreement with airborne LiDAR DTM-based corrections at the beam level, not the footprint level](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09854)

Result: 解析错误

Abstract: The Global Ecosystem Dynamics Investigation (GEDI) mission measures three-dimensional forest structure with a LiDAR aboard the International Space Station. GEDI observations have geolocation errors of about 10 m, which cause misalignment with other remote sensing data and reduce the accuracy of downstream tasks. Matching GEDI ground elevations against high-resolution digital terrain models (DTMs) from airborne LiDAR is one way to correct these errors, but such DTMs exist in few regions. To widen this method's applicability, we super-resolved a more widely available low-resolution DTM and used it for the matching. Our question is whether a super-resolved DTM yields the same correction as a 1 m-resolution airborne LiDAR DTM and, if not, why. In steep forests of western Japan, we super-resolved an existing 10 m-resolution DTM to 1 m. Improving its accuracy required fusion with a global 30 m-resolution DTM from a different source. Using the resulting DTM, we corrected GEDI observation positions first by a single shift per ground track, then per observation. For most tracks, the shifts nearly matched those from the airborne LiDAR DTM, likely because DTM errors canceled out across many observations. Per-observation corrections, however, did not match. At a single observation, the error does not cancel out and is too large for the correction. This error likely originates mainly from the input DTM. Therefore, even without a 1 m-resolution DTM, a roughly 10 m-resolution DTM, fused with a global DTM and super-resolved to 1 m, should yield track-wise corrections nearly matching those from a 1 m-resolution DTM.

</details>
